-Section
4.4
, we conclude that both SS-SN test statistics offer very stable and relatively accurate size across a wide range of data generating processes for most combinations of
(n,p,\rho)
we examined, as compared to the traditional bandwidth-free tests. The latter often yield very large size distortion in the case of small sample size and/or large dimension when the magnitude of temporal dependence is moderate. The size stability and accuracy with respect to the dimension and magnitude of dependence is a major gain of the SS-SN procedures.
As a consequence of the usual size-power trade-off, there is a power loss for SS-SN tests due to the use of sample splitting. However, when comparing the optimal SS-SN test to the traditional bandwidth-free counterparts (e.g.,
SS\text{-}SN_{P}
for dense alternative, and
SS\text{-}SN_{1}
for sparse alternative), the power loss is mild. In a sense, this is similar to the “more accurate size but less power” phenomenon when comparing tests based on fixed-
b
asymptotics versus small-
b
asymptotics [
Kiefer and
Vogelsang 2005
].
In practice, when there is no prior knowledge about the type of alternative, we recommend the user to employ the Bonferonni test, i.e.,
SS\text{-}SN_{b}
by setting
\alpha=0.5
. The asymptotic independence between
L_{2}
-type and
L_{\infty}
-type SS-SN test statistics as stated in Theorem
7
further lends theoretical support to the Bonferroni test as it is expected to be non-conservative when the dimension of the parameter is moderate and sample size is large.

(a)

(b)

(c)

(d)

(e)

(f)

Figure 3
:
Size adjusted power for testing hypothesis on multivariate mean (first row), in a regression model (second row) and the existence of a change point (last row) under the dense (left column) and sparse (right column) alternatives

5
Conclusion

In this article, we propose a class of new tests for hypotheses on a multi-dimensional parameter based on SN and sample splitting. Our two SS-SN statistics do not involve any bandwidth parameter and the asymptotic null distribution is pivotal and is independent of the sample splitting proportion
\alpha
. The construction of both SS-SN statistics are rather straightforward and the test statistics applied to the second part of sample
{\cal P}_{2}
after dimension reduction based on the first part
{\cal P}_{1}
are effectively targeting at parameter of dimension one. This sample splitting approach is broadly applicable to many time series testing problems, and we only cover testing hypotheses on marginal means, autocorrelations, regression parameter and a change point in multivariate mean to illustrate its usefulness. Overall, the SS-SN methodology provides an important addition to the existing SN toolbox owing to its superior ability of dealing with moderate dimensional parameter in the inference of low or moderate dimensional time series.

Below we shall highlight several appealing features of our test statistics. (a) For a moderate dimensional parameter, the size of our test statistics is considerably more accurate than traditional SN statistic, especially when temporal dependence is strong. As a price to pay, the SS-SN test loses some power. However, the power loss is moderate as seen from both theoretical power analysis and simulation studies. In practice, we recommend the practitioner to set
\alpha=0.5
, and use the Bonferroni test that combines the two SS-SN test statistics so the power is adaptive to both sparse and dense alternatives. Simulation results show that the Bonferroni test exhibits accurate size and all-round good power in all settings.
(b) We managed to show the asymptotic validity of
SS\text{-}SN_{1}
and
SS\text{-}SN_{P}
test statistics and their asymptotic independence under the null in multivariate mean testing problem in a growing-dimensional setting, which is an interesting theoretical contribution to the SN literature. The theory is consistent with the empirical observation that the size is robust for SS-SN test statistics for a broad range of dimensions.
(c) As a by-product of dimension reduction involved in SS-SN, there is substantial saving in computational cost as compared to traditional SN test statistics. In the mean testing problem, the cost of our SS-SN test statistics scales linearly in
p
, which is superior to that for the traditional SN statistic.

To conclude, we mention some possible extensions. The scope of this paper can be considerably expanded by using the GMM framework of
Kiefer and
Vogelsang 2005
. Also one can regard KVB’s test as a special case of the so-called fixed-
b
asymptotics (
Kiefer and
Vogelsang 2005
) with
b=1
and the use of Bartlett kernel. It is expected that the fixed-
b
based tests and also other fixed-smoothing based tests as advocated in
Sun 2014b
,
Hwang and
Sun 2017
,
Wang and Sun 2020
, will encounter the same size distortion problem when the dimension is moderate and temporal dependence is moderate/strong. Hence it would be interesting to extend the SS-SN idea to fixed-smoothing methods and to GMM settings. These topics are left for future research.

Acknowledgement:

We would like to thank the Associate Editor and two reviewers, whose thoughtful comments led to a substantial improvement of the paper.

is contained in the dataset
NelPlo
in the R package
tseries

(https://cran.r-project.org/web/packages/tseries/index.html).

SUPPLEMENT TO “Another Look at Bandwidth-free Inference: a Sample Splitting Approach”

BY Yi Zhang AND Xiaofeng Shao

The supplement is organized as follows. In Appendix
A
, we compare different SS-SN statistics where the projection vectors are defined using different rescaling methods. In Appendix
B
, we use a real data example to illustrate the finite sample performance of our SS-SN tests for zero autocorrelation. In Appendix
C
, we provide the proofs for all main results presented in the paper. In Appendix
D
, we collect all auxiliary lemmas and their proofs.

Appendix A
Comparison of SS-SN Statistics Defined Using Different Rescaling Methods

A.1
Variants of SS-SN Statistics Based on Different Rescaling Methods

In Sections
2.1
and
2.2
, the projection vectors
\mathbf{e}_{\hat{j}}
and
\mathbf{\hat{P}}
are calculated on the first subsample
{\cal P}_{1}
and an important step involved is the rescaling, which is conducted separately for each dimension using the sample marginal variance. As pointed out by a referee, this may not be the best approach. In this section, we introduce four alternative rescaling methods, define their corresponding
L_{\infty}
-type and
L_{2}
-type SS-SN statistics and compare their finite sample performances.
We denote the
L_{\infty}
-type and
L_{2}
-type SS-SN statistics defined in Sections
2.1
and
2.2
as
SS\text{-}SN_{1}
and
SS\text{-}SN_{P}
.

For
j=1,2,\dots,p
, let
\hat{\Gamma}_{j}
be the kernel estimator of the long run variance of
j
th component series and
\boldsymbol{\hat{\Gamma}}
be the kernel estimator of the long run covariance matrix. For consistent long run variance (matrix) estimation, we use the Bartlett kernel [
Newey and
West 1987
] and select the bandwidth according to the data dependent method proposed in
Newey and
West 1994
. Note that we only use
{\cal P}_{1}
for the estimation.

Denote
V^{(M)}_{n}(j)=(n{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor})^{-2}\sum_{k={\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}+1}^{n}({S}^{j}_{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}+1,k}{-}\frac{k-{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}{n-{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}{S}^{j}_{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}+1,n})^{2}
and
V_{n}(\boldsymbol{\hat{P}})=(n-{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor})^{-2}\sum_{k={\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}+1}^{n}\Big\{\boldsymbol{\hat{P}}^{\top}\big[\mathbf{S}_{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}+1,k}-\frac{k-{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}{n-{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}\mathbf{S}_{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}+1,n}\big]\Big\}^{2}
then the first two variants of SS-SN statistics are defined as

\displaystyle SSSN\text{-}LM_{1}=

\displaystyle\frac{(n{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor})^{{-}1}({S}^{\hat{j}_{LM}}_{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}+1,n}{-}(n{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor})\mu_{0}^{\hat{j}_{LM}})^{2}}{V^{(M)}_{n}(\hat{j}_{LM})},

\displaystyle SSSN\text{-}LM_{P}=

\displaystyle\frac{(n-{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor})^{-1}\Big\{\boldsymbol{\hat{P}}_{LM}^{\top}\big[\mathbf{S}_{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}+1,n}{-}(n{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor})\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}\big]\Big\}^{2}}{V_{n}(\boldsymbol{\hat{P}}_{LM})}

with

\displaystyle\hat{j}_{LM}=

\displaystyle\argmax_{j=1,2,\dots,p}\frac{n^{-1}({S}^{j}_{1,{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}{\mu}^{j}_{0})^{2}}{\hat{\Gamma}_{j}},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\hat{P}}_{LM}=

\displaystyle diag\big\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{\hat{\Gamma}_{1}}},\dots,\frac{1}{\sqrt{\hat{\Gamma}_{p}}}\big\}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}(\boldsymbol{S}_{1,{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}-{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}).

Note that
\hat{j}_{LM}
and
\boldsymbol{\hat{P}}_{LM}
are rescaled separately for each dimension using the corresponding consistent long run variance estimator. Similarly, we can rescale the projection vectors using the consistent long run covariance matrix estimator
\boldsymbol{\hat{\Gamma}}
and define

\displaystyle SSSN\text{-}L_{1}=

\displaystyle\frac{(n{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor})^{{-}1}({S}^{\hat{j}_{L}}_{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}+1,n}{-}(n{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor})\mu_{0}^{\hat{j}_{L}})^{2}}{V^{(M)}_{n}(\hat{j}_{L})},

\displaystyle SSSN\text{-}L_{P}=

\displaystyle\frac{(n-{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor})^{-1}\Big\{\boldsymbol{\hat{P}}_{L}^{\top}\big[\mathbf{S}_{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}+1,n}{-}(n{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor})\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}\big]\Big\}^{2}}{V_{n}(\boldsymbol{\hat{P}}_{L})}

with

\displaystyle\hat{j}_{L}=

\displaystyle\argmax_{j=1,2,\dots,p}\big|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\mathbf{e}_{j}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\hat{\Gamma}}^{-1/2}(\boldsymbol{S}_{1,{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0})\big|,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\hat{P}}_{L}=

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\hat{\Gamma}}^{-1/2}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}(\boldsymbol{S}_{1,{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}).

Note that
\boldsymbol{\hat{P}}_{L}
is the estimator of the optimal projection (see Remark
4
in Section
2.2
). The above rescaling methods involve the choice of bandwidth parameters, which is a bit contrary to the spirit of bandwidth-free testing. Alternatively, we can introduce new
L_{\infty}
-type and
L_{2}
-type statistics with the projection vectors rescaled using SN method. Denote
\tilde{V}^{(M)}_{n}(j)={\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}^{-2}\sum_{k=1}^{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}({S}^{j}_{1,k}{-}\frac{k}{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}{S}^{j}_{1,{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}})^{2}
and define

\displaystyle SSSN\text{-}SNM_{1}=

\displaystyle\frac{(n{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor})^{{-}1}({S}^{\hat{j}_{SNM}}_{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}+1,n}{-}(n{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor})\mu_{0}^{\hat{j}_{SNM}})^{2}}{V^{(M)}_{n}(\hat{j}_{SNM})},

\displaystyle SSSN\text{-}SNM_{P}=

\displaystyle\frac{(n-{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor})^{-1}\Big\{\boldsymbol{\hat{P}}_{SNM}^{\top}\big[\mathbf{S}_{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}+1,n}{-}(n{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor})\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}\big]\Big\}^{2}}{V_{n}(\boldsymbol{\hat{P}}_{SNM})}

with

\displaystyle\hat{j}_{SNM}=

\displaystyle\argmax_{j=1,2,\dots,p}\frac{n^{-1}({S}^{j}_{1,{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}{\mu}^{j}_{0})^{2}}{\tilde{V}^{(M)}_{n}(j)},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\hat{P}}_{SNM}=

\displaystyle diag\big\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{\tilde{V}^{(M)}_{n}(1)}},\dots,\frac{1}{\sqrt{\tilde{V}^{(M)}_{n}(p)}}\big\}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}(\boldsymbol{S}_{1,{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}-{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}).

Note that the rescaling involved in forming
\hat{j}_{SNM}
and
\boldsymbol{\hat{P}}_{SNM}
is done via the self-normalizer
\{\tilde{V}^{(M)}_{n}(j)\}
for each dimension. Similarly we can rescale them using the SN matrix
\boldsymbol{\tilde{V}}_{n}={\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}^{-2}\sum_{t=1}^{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}\{\boldsymbol{S}_{1,t}-(t/{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor})\boldsymbol{S}_{1,{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}\}\{\boldsymbol{S}_{1,t}-(t/{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor})\boldsymbol{S}_{1,{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}\}^{\top}
and define

\displaystyle SSSN\text{-}SN_{1}=

\displaystyle\frac{(n{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor})^{{-}1}({S}^{\hat{j}_{SN}}_{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}+1,n}{-}(n{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor})\mu_{0}^{\hat{j}_{SN}})^{2}}{V^{(M)}_{n}(\hat{j}_{SN})},

\displaystyle SSSN\text{-}SN_{P}=

\displaystyle\frac{(n-{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor})^{-1}\Big\{\boldsymbol{\hat{P}}_{SN}^{\top}\big[\mathbf{S}_{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}+1,n}{-}(n{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor})\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}\big]\Big\}^{2}}{V_{n}(\boldsymbol{\hat{P}}_{SN})}

with

\displaystyle\hat{j}_{SN}=

\displaystyle\argmax_{j=1,2,\dots,p}\big|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\mathbf{e}_{j}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\tilde{V}}_{n}^{-1/2}(\boldsymbol{S}_{1,{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0})\big|,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\hat{P}}_{SN}=

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\tilde{V}}_{n}^{-1/2}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}(\boldsymbol{S}_{1,{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}).

The asymptotic property for these variants of SS-SN test statistics can be obtained by following a similar argument used in the proofs of Theorem
1
and Theorem
2
when the dimension
p
is fixed. The details are omitted.
The limiting null distributions for all these variants of SS-SN statistics are still
U_{1}
so we can construct the test by using the tabulated critical values for
U_{1}
.

A.2
Empirical Size Comparison

To examine the empirical size of different SS-SN statistics, we focus on the testing of multivariate mean. Under the null, we assume the data comes from the following VAR(1) model:

\mathbf{X}_{t}=\rho\mathbf{I}_{p}\mathbf{X}_{t-1}+\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{t},
where
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle iid}}{{\sim}}N(\boldsymbol{0},\mathbf{\Sigma}_{p})
. We consider four DGPs with different dependence structures: for DGP1 we let
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{p}=\mathbf{I}_{p}
; for DGP2 we let
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{p}=[\Sigma_{i,j}]_{i,j}=[0.7^{|i-j|}]_{i,j}
; for DGP3 we let
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{p}=[\Sigma_{i,j}]_{i,j}=[(-0.7)^{|i-j|}]_{i,j}
; for DGP4 we set
\mathbf{\Sigma}_{p}=0.4\mathbf{I}_{p}+0.6\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\top}
. Here DGP1, DGP2
\&
3 and DGP4 correspond to independent, weakly dependent and strongly dependent components for
\{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{t}\}
and
\{\mathbf{X}_{t}\}
respectively. We set the nominal level at
5\%
and the experiment is repeated 3000 times with the length of time series
n\in\{100,300\}
,
\rho\in\{-0.7,-0.5,0.2,0.5,0.7\}
and
p\in\{5,10\}
. We also include the test statistic used in
Lobato 2001
(denoted as Lobato) as comparison. We only show the result for the splitting ratio
\alpha=0.5
and qualitatively similar results hold for
\alpha=0.15,0.3
. The original
L_{\infty}
-type and
L_{2}
-type SS-SN statistics are denoted as
M_{1}
and
M_{P}
while all other newly defined SS-SN statistics are denoted using their corresponding suffixes.

As Table
5
and
6
show, all the SS-SN statistics have similar empirical sizes, which are much more accurate than the traditional SN statistic. Also the size stability across
p
and
\rho
for these SS-SN test statistics, as compared to Lobato, are very noticeable.

A.3
Size Adjusted Power Comparison for Multivariate Mean Testing

For the size adjusted power, we consider five alternatives. For ALT1, ALT2 and ALT3 we assume
\{\mathbf{X}_{t}{-}\boldsymbol{\mu}\}
are generated from DGP1, DGP2 and DGP3 as in Appendix
A.2
with
\rho=0.7
(we have also run simulations with
\rho=0.2
and the results are qualitatively similar). For ALT4, we assume
\mathbf{X}_{t}{-}\boldsymbol{\mu}=\boldsymbol{\rho}_{1}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{t-1}+\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{t},\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle iid}}{{\sim}}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{1}\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbf{I}_{p})
with
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{1}=diag\{{-}0.8,{-}0.8,{-}0.9,{-}0.8,{-}0.8,\dots\}
and
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{1}=diag\{1,1,2,1,1,\dots\}
. For ALT5, we assume
\mathbf{X}_{t}{-}\boldsymbol{\mu}=\boldsymbol{\rho}_{2}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{t-1}+\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{t},\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle iid}}{{\sim}}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2}\mathcal{N}(0,\mathbf{I}_{p})
with
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{2}=diag\{{-}0.8,{-}0.8,\frac{\sqrt{10}}{5}{-}1,{-}0.8,{-}0.8,\dots\}
and
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{2}=diag\{1,1,\frac{\sqrt{10}}{10},1,1,\dots\}
. Note that for ALT4 and ALT5, the marginal long run variances all equal to
\frac{1}{25}
, but the marginal variances are not equal. Except for the third component, all marginal variances are equal to 1, while the marginal variance for the third component is 4 in ALT4 and
\frac{1}{10}
in ALT5.

For each alternative, we consider both sparse and dense cases. Under the sparse alternative, we assume
\boldsymbol{\mu}=\mu\mathbf{e}_{3}
and under the dense alternative we assume
\boldsymbol{\mu}=\mu\boldsymbol{1}
. We set
n=300
,
p=20

\alpha=0.5
and nominal level at
5\%
, and the experiment is repeated 2000 times. The power curves for different alternatives are shown in Fig.
4
-
5
. Since the
L_{2}
-type statistic generally performs better under the dense alternative and
L_{\infty}
-type statistic performs better under sparse alternative, we only plot the power curves for the
L_{\infty}
-type (
L_{2}
-type) SS-SN statistics under the sparse (dense) alternative to allow easy visualization.

A.4
Size Adjusted Power Comparison for Change Point Testing

To examine the size adjusted power of different SS-SN statistics, we further consider the change point testing problem under the ALTs of Appendix
A.3
. Assume the time series data
\mathbf{Y}_{t}
comes from the following model:

\mathbf{Y}_{t}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{X}_{t},&1\leq t\leq k_{0}={\lfloor nr_{0}\rfloor}\\
\mathbf{X}_{t}+\boldsymbol{\mu}&k_{0}<t\leq n,\end{cases}

where
\mathbf{X}_{t}
is generated according to the five DGPs in Appendix
A.3
. For the sparse alternative, we let
\boldsymbol{\mu}=\mu\mathbf{e}_{3}
and for the dense alternative, we let
\boldsymbol{\mu}=\mu\boldsymbol{1}
. We set
r_{0}=1/2
, the trimming parameter
b=0.15
and the experiment is repeated 2000 times. As a comparison, we also plot the power curve of the statistic proposed in
Shao and
Zhang 2010
(denoted as SZ).

A.5
Summary of Simulation Results for Different SS-SN Statistics

As Tables
5
and
6
show, all the SS-SN statistics have similar empirical sizes for all DGPs. The size accuracy and stability across
p
and
\rho
, as compared to Lobato, is a main advantage. This allows us to focus on the power comparison to understand which SS-SN test works better and in what situations.

According to size adjusted power curves presented in the four Figures (i.e., Fig.
4
–
7
), the power performance of SS-SN statistics highly depend on the alternatives and data generating processes. There is no SS-SN test statistic that dominates all others in power.
For the SS-SN statistics rescaled by the long run covariance matrix estimator (
SSSN\text{-}L_{1}
and
SSSN\text{-}L_{P}
) or the SN matrix (
SSSN\text{-}SN_{1}
and
SSSN\text{-}SN_{P}
), the power loss are larger compared with most marginally rescaled SS-SN statistics when there is no or weak cross-sectional dependence (see ALT1 size adjusted power in Fig.
4(a)
,
4(b)
,
6(a)
and
6(b)
). When the cross-sectional dependence is strong (ALT2 and ALT3),
SSSN\text{-}L_{1}
and
SSSN\text{-}L_{P}
, as well as
SSSN\text{-}SN_{1}
and
SSSN\text{-}SN_{P}
, still have larger power loss in dense case of ALT2 (see Fig.
4(c)
and
6(c)
). While in ALT3, they have smaller power loss under the dense alternative and the power curves are similar to other SS-SN statistics under the sparse alternative (see Fig.
4(e)
,
4(f)
,
6(e)
and
6(f)
).

For the SS-SN statistics rescaled marginally by the marginal variance estimator (
SS\text{-}SN_{1}
and
SS\text{-}SN_{P}
), long run variance estimator (
SSSN\text{-}LM_{1}
and
SSSN\text{-}LM_{P}
) and the marginal self-normalizer (
SSSN\text{-}SNM_{1}
and
SSSN\text{-}SNM_{P}
),
SSSN\text{-}SNM_{1}
and
SSSN\text{-}SNM_{P}
have noticeably larger power loss compared with other marginally rescaled SS-SN statistics for ALT1, ALT2 and ALT3, whereas for ALT4 and ALT5, they have similar power curves as
SS\text{-}SN_{1}
and
SS\text{-}SN_{P}
.
SS\text{-}SN_{1}
and
SS\text{-}SN_{P}
outperform
SSSN\text{-}LM_{1}
and
SSSN\text{-}LM_{P}
in all ALTs and in some settings the improvement is more pronounced (see Fig.
5(b)
,
5(d)
,
6(b)
,
6(d)
,
6(f)
,
7(b)
and
7(d)
). In the sparse case of ALT4 (see Fig.
5(b)
and
7(b)
),
SSSN\text{-}LM_{1}
has large power loss compared with
SS\text{-}SN_{1}
although ALT4 appears more favorable to
SSSN\text{-}LM_{1}
. This may be due to the intrinsic difficulty of long run variance estimation, especially when the underlying process is not autoregressive and temporal dependence is strong.

Given these empirical observations, we chose to adopt
SS\text{-}SN_{1}
and
SS\text{-}SN_{P}
in Sections
2.1
and
2.2
, for its simplicity and bandwidth-free nature. This is also supported by the simulation results presented here, as they compare quite well relative to other marginally rescaled SS-SN statistics.
When compared to the rescaling methods using the
p
-dimensional long run covariance matrix estimator or the SN matrix,
SS\text{-}SN_{1}
and
SS\text{-}SN_{P}
have less power loss in most of the ALTs. In addition, the asymptotic properties for
SS\text{-}SN_{1}
and
SS\text{-}SN_{P}
are easier to analyze, especially in the growing dimensional setting.

Appendix B
Empirical Example

In this section, we consider a real data example where we test for zero autocorrelations of a univariate time series. The data comes from the extended Nelson and Plosser Economic Dataset, which contains fifteen annual economic time series for the U.S. economy. We present the result for the growth rate of consumer price index (CPI), which covers the period from 1861 to 1988. There are 128 observations in total and Fig.
8
plots the CPI growth rate series.

Figure 8
:
Annual U.S. CPI growth rate

Consider testing the null hypothesis
H_{0}:r_{1}=r_{2}=\cdots=r_{p}=0
for
p\in\{1,5,10,20\}
. The sample autocorrelations,
\hat{r}_{p}=\hat{\gamma}_{p}/\hat{\gamma}_{0}
, where
\hat{\gamma}_{p}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{t=1}^{n-p}(X_{t}-\bar{X}_{n})(X_{t+p}-\bar{X}_{n})
are shown in the ACF plot in Fig.
9
. It appears that
\hat{r}_{1}=0.61
and
|\hat{r}_{p}|<0.21
for
p\geq 2
. The Ljung-Box test gives a
p
-value less than
2.99\times 10^{-12}
when
p=1
, which is a strong indication that the null hypothesis
H_{0}:r_{1}=0
does not hold. Note that the limiting null distribution of Ljung-Box test statistic is
\chi^{2}
and is obtained under the strong iid assumption, whereas the test developed by
Lobato 2001
and ours can accommodate higher-order dependence under the null of zero autocorrelations.

The values for the
SS\text{-}SN_{1}
,
SS\text{-}SN_{P}
statistics are shown in Table
7
, which also includes the test statistic used in
Lobato 2001
(denoted as Lobato) as a comparison. For
p\in\{1,5,10\}
, all SS-SN statistics reject the null at level
10\%
and all SS-SN statistics with
\alpha=0.15
reject the null at level
5\%
. For
p=20
,
SS\text{-}SN_{1}
with
\alpha=0.15
rejects the null at level
5\%
while the
SS\text{-}SN_{P}
does not reject the null for all
\alpha
s. This may be due to the ”sparsity” of the alternative since only the sample autocorrelation at lag one appears to be significantly different from zero. For Lobato’s test, the null hypothesis is rejected at level
10\%
only when
p=1
, which is consistent with the simulation result in Table
2
. The intuition is that Lobato is severely under-sized when
p
is large/moderate, suggesting that the critical value based on the limiting null distribution is too large when
p
is moderate, resulting in low power in this setting.

Figure 9
:
Sample ACF of the CPI growth rate series

Appendix C
Proofs of Main Results

C.1
Proof of Theorem
1

Without loss of generality, we assume
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}=\boldsymbol{0}
. For
j=1,2,\dots,p
, denote
S_{j}=n^{-1}(S_{1,{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}^{j})^{2}/\sigma_{j}^{2}
,
\hat{k}=\argmax_{k=1,2,\dots,p}S_{k}
and let
S_{(1)}\leq S_{(2)}\leq\cdots\leq S_{(p)}
be the order statistics of
\{S_{1},S_{2},\dots,S_{p}\}
.

First we prove part (i) of Theorem
1
. It suffices to show
P(\hat{j}\neq\hat{k})\to 0
and
T^{(M)}_{n}(\alpha,\hat{k})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{\to}}U_{1}
. So we divide the proof into two parts:

(a)
P(\hat{j}\neq\hat{k})\to 0
.

(b)
T^{(M)}_{n}(\alpha,\hat{k})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{\to}}U_{1}
.

Proof of part (a)
: Note that for fixed
\epsilon>0
,

\displaystyle P(\hat{k}=\hat{j};S_{(p)}{>}S_{(p{-}1)}{+}\epsilon)\leq P(\hat{k}=\hat{j})\leq 1

(24)

Denote
B^{(j)}(r)=\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{B}_{p}(r)
, and
\mathcal{W}_{j}=B^{(j)}(\alpha)/\sigma_{j}^{2}
with order statistics
\mathcal{W}_{(1)}\leq\mathcal{W}_{(2)}\leq\cdots\leq\mathcal{W}_{(p)}
. By the continuous mapping theorem, we have
P(S_{(p)}{>}S_{(p-1)}{+}\epsilon)\to P(\mathcal{W}_{(p)}{>}\mathcal{W}_{(p-1)}{+}\epsilon)
as
n\to\infty
. Note that

\displaystyle P(\hat{k}=\hat{j};S_{(p)}{>}S_{(p-1)}{+}\epsilon)=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{p}P(\hat{k}=\hat{j}=j;S_{(p)}{>}S_{(p-1)}{+}\epsilon)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{p}\Big[P(\hat{j}=j;S_{(p)}{>}S_{(p-1)}{+}\epsilon){-}P(\hat{k}\neq j;\hat{j}=j;S_{(p)}{>}S_{(p-1)}{+}\epsilon)\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle P(S_{(p)}{>}S_{(p-1)}{+}\epsilon)-\sum_{j=1}^{p}P(\hat{k}\neq j;\hat{j}=j;S_{(p)}{>}S_{(p-1)}{+}\epsilon).

(25)

Fix
j\in\{1,2,\dots,p\}
, from Assumption
2
we have
\max_{j=1,2,\dots,p}|\sigma_{j}^{2}/\hat{\sigma}_{j}^{2}-1|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}0
, so

\displaystyle P(\hat{k}\neq j;\hat{j}=j;S_{(p)}{>}S_{(p-1)}{+}\epsilon)\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in\{1,2,\dots,p\};i\neq j}P(S_{j}{>}S_{i}{+}\epsilon;\frac{\sigma_{j}^{2}S_{j}}{\hat{\sigma}_{j}^{2}}\leq\frac{\sigma_{i}^{2}S_{i}}{\hat{\sigma}_{i}^{2}})

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle 0,

(26)

and from Equation (
C.1
) we have
P(\hat{k}=\hat{j};S_{(p)}{>}S_{(p-1)}{+}\epsilon)\to P(\mathcal{W}_{(p)}{>}\mathcal{W}_{(p-1)}{+}\epsilon)
as
n\to\infty
. So from Equation (
24
),

P(\mathcal{W}_{(p)}{>}\mathcal{W}_{(p-1)}{+}\epsilon)\leq\liminf P(\hat{k}=\hat{j})\leq 1

and part (a) is proved by letting
\epsilon\to 0
.

Proof of part (b)
: By Lemma
1
, the continuous mapping theorem and Assumption
1
, we have

\displaystyle\hat{k}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{\to}}\argmax_{j=1,2,\dots,p}\frac{\big\{B^{(j)}(\alpha)\big\}^{2}}{\sigma_{j}^{2}}=\tilde{k},

(27)

\displaystyle T^{(M)}_{n}(\alpha,\hat{k})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{\to}}\frac{\big\{B^{(\tilde{k})}(1)-B^{(\tilde{k})}(\alpha)\big\}^{2}}{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\int_{\alpha}^{1}\big\{B^{(\tilde{k})}(r)-\frac{1-r}{1-\alpha}B^{(\tilde{k})}(\alpha)-\frac{r-\alpha}{1-\alpha}B^{(\tilde{k})}(1)\big\}^{2}dr}=\tilde{T},

(28)

Since for any
t\in[0,\alpha]
,
r\in[\alpha,1]
and
j,k\in\{1,2,\dots,p\}
:

\cov[(B^{(j)}(r)-B^{(j)}(\alpha)),B^{(k)}(t)]=(\min\{r,t\}-\min\{\alpha,t\})T_{jk}=0,

(29)

we have
\{B^{(j)}(r)-B^{(j)}(\alpha)\}_{r\in(\alpha,1],j\in\{1,\dots,p\}}
and
\{B^{(k)}(t)\}_{t\in[0,\alpha],k\in\{1,\dots,p\}}
are independent. Then the conditional distribution of
\tilde{T}
given
\tilde{k}
is:

\displaystyle\tilde{T}\Big|\tilde{k}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\frac{\big\{B^{(\tilde{k})}(1)-B^{(\tilde{k})}(\alpha)\big\}^{2}}{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\int_{\alpha}^{1}\big\{B^{(\tilde{k})}(r)-\frac{1-r}{1-\alpha}B^{(\tilde{k})}(\alpha)-\frac{r-\alpha}{1-\alpha}B^{(\tilde{k})}(1)\big\}^{2}dr}\Big|\tilde{k}

(30)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\frac{\big\{\frac{B^{(\tilde{k})}(1)}{\sqrt{\Gamma_{\tilde{k}\tilde{k}}}}-\frac{B^{(\tilde{k})}(\alpha)}{\sqrt{\Gamma_{\tilde{k}\tilde{k}}}}\big\}^{2}}{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\int_{\alpha}^{1}\big\{\frac{B^{(\tilde{k})}(r)}{\sqrt{\Gamma_{\tilde{k}\tilde{k}}}}-\frac{1-r}{1-\alpha}\frac{B^{(\tilde{k})}(\alpha)}{\sqrt{\Gamma_{\tilde{k}\tilde{k}}}}-\frac{r-\alpha}{1-\alpha}\frac{B^{(\tilde{k})}(1)}{\sqrt{\Gamma_{\tilde{k}\tilde{k}}}}\big\}^{2}dr}\Big|\tilde{k}

(31)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\frac{\big\{B(1)-B(\alpha)\big\}^{2}}{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\int_{\alpha}^{1}\big\{B(r)-\frac{1-r}{1-\alpha}B(\alpha)-\frac{r-\alpha}{1-\alpha}B(1)\big\}^{2}dr}

(32)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\frac{\big\{B(1)-B(\alpha)\big\}^{2}}{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\int_{\alpha}^{1}\big\{B(r)-B(\alpha)-\frac{r-\alpha}{1-\alpha}(B(1)-B(\alpha))\big\}^{2}dr}

(33)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\frac{\big\{B(1)-B(\alpha)\big\}^{2}}{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\int_{0}^{1-\alpha}\big\{B(r+\alpha)-B(\alpha)-\frac{r+\alpha-\alpha}{1-\alpha}(B(1)-B(\alpha))\big\}^{2}dr}

(34)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\frac{\big\{B(1-\alpha)\big\}^{2}}{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\int_{0}^{1-\alpha}\big\{B(r)-\frac{r}{1-\alpha}B(1-\alpha)\big\}^{2}dr}

(35)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\frac{\big\{B(1-\alpha)\big\}^{2}}{\int_{0}^{1}\big\{B((1-\alpha)r)-rB(1-\alpha)\big\}^{2}dr}

(36)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\frac{\big\{B(1)\big\}^{2}}{\int_{0}^{1}\big\{B(r)-rB(1)\big\}^{2}dr}.

(37)

For Equation (
32
), we used the fact that
\tilde{k}
is a function of
\{B^{(k)}(t)\}_{t\in[0,\alpha],k\in\{1,\dots,p\}}
, which is independent of
\{B^{(j)}(r)-B^{(j)}(\alpha)\}_{r\in(\alpha,1],j\in\{1,\dots,p\}}
. For Equation (
35
), we used the property
\{B(r+\alpha)-B(\alpha):r\in[0,1-\alpha]\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\{B(r):r\in[0,1-\alpha]\}
. For Equations (
34
) and (
36
), we used the change of variable property for integration. For Equation (
37
), we used the property
\{B((1-\alpha)r):r\in[0,1]\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\{\sqrt{1-\alpha}B(r):r\in[0,1]\}
.

So the limiting null distribution is

\tilde{T}=\frac{\big\{B(1)\big\}^{2}}{\int_{0}^{1}\big\{B(r)-rB(1)\big\}^{2}dr}=U_{1},

(38)

and part (i) is proved.

To prove part (ii).1 of Theorem
1
, note that there exist a sequence
\{j_{n}\}\in\{1,2,\dots,p\}
such that
\sqrt{n}|\mu_{n}^{j_{n}}|\to\infty
. Denote
A^{\alpha}(j)=n^{-{1/2}}[\mathbf{e}_{j}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{S}_{1,{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n})]
, by the definition of
\hat{j}
we have

\displaystyle\frac{\max\limits_{j=1,2,\dots,p}[A^{\alpha}(j)]^{2}{+}2\max\limits_{j=1,2,\dots,p}|A^{\alpha}(j)|\frac{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}{\sqrt{n}}|\mu_{n}^{\hat{j}}|{+}\frac{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}^{2}}{n}[\mu_{n}^{\hat{j}}]^{2}}{\min\limits_{j=1,2,\dots,p}\hat{\sigma}_{j}^{2}}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{n^{-1}[\mathbf{e}_{\hat{j}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{S}_{1,{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}]^{2}}{\hat{\sigma}_{\hat{j}}^{2}}\geq\frac{n^{-1}[\mathbf{e}_{j_{n}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{S}_{1,{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}]^{2}}{\hat{\sigma}_{j_{n}}^{2}}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{\min\limits_{j=1,2,\dots,p}[A^{\alpha}(j)]^{2}{-}2\max\limits_{j=1,2,\dots,p}|A^{\alpha}(j)|\frac{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}{\sqrt{n}}|\mu_{n}^{j_{n}}|{+}\frac{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}^{2}}{n}[\mu_{n}^{j_{n}}]^{2}}{\max\limits_{j=1,2,\dots,p}\hat{\sigma}_{j}^{2}}.

(39)

Since
\{\max\limits_{j=1,2,\dots,p}\hat{\sigma}_{j}^{2}\}^{-1}
,
\max\limits_{j=1,2,\dots,p}|A^{\alpha}(j)|
and
\min\limits_{j=1,2,\dots,p}[A^{\alpha}(j)]^{2}
are all of the order
O_{p}(1)
, the right hand side of Inequality (
39
) diverges to
\infty
in probability. Since
\max\limits_{j=1,2,\dots,p}[A^{\alpha}(j)]^{2}
and
\{\min\limits_{j=1,2,\dots,p}\hat{\sigma}_{j}^{2}\}^{-1}
are also of order
O_{p}(1)
, we have that
\sqrt{n}|\mu_{n}^{\hat{j}}|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}\infty
. Denote
D^{\alpha}(j)=(n{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor})^{-1/2}[{S}^{j}_{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}+1,n}{-}(n{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor})\mu_{n}^{j}]
and we have

\displaystyle T^{(M)}_{n}(\alpha,\hat{j})

\displaystyle=\frac{[D^{\alpha}(\hat{j})]^{2}+2D^{\alpha}(\hat{j})\sqrt{n-{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}\mu_{n}^{\hat{j}}{+}(n{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor})(\mu_{n}^{\hat{j}})^{2}}{V^{(M)}_{n}(\hat{j})}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\min\limits_{j=1,2,\dots,p}[D^{\alpha}(j)]^{2}{-}2\max\limits_{j=1,2,\dots,p}|D^{\alpha}(j)|\sqrt{n{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}|\mu_{n}^{\hat{j}}|+(n-{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor})|\mu_{n}^{\hat{j}}|^{2}}{\max\limits_{j=1,2,\dots,p}V^{(M)}_{n}(j)}.

(40)

Since
[\max\limits_{j=1,2,\dots,p}V^{(M)}_{n}(j)]^{-1}
,
\min\limits_{j=1,2,\dots,p}[D^{\alpha}(j)]^{2}
and
\max\limits_{j=1,2,\dots,p}|D^{\alpha}(j)|
are of the order
O_{p}(1)
and
\sqrt{n-{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}|\mu_{n}^{\hat{j}}|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}\infty
, we have
T^{(M)}_{n}(\alpha,\hat{j})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}\infty
and part (ii).1 of Theorem
1
is proved.

To prove part (ii).2 of Theorem
1
, following the same idea as in the proof of part (i), we only need to show

\hat{k}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{\to}}\argmax_{j=1,2,\dots,p}\frac{\big\{B^{(j)}(\alpha)+\alpha c^{j}\big\}^{2}}{\sigma_{j}^{2}}\mbox{ and }T^{(M)}_{n}(\alpha,\hat{k})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{\to}}U^{\ast}.

By continuous mapping theorem,

\displaystyle\hat{k}=\argmax_{j=1,2,\dots,p}\frac{n^{-1}[S^{j}_{1,{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}-{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}\mu_{n}^{j}+{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}\mu_{n}^{j}]^{2}}{\sigma_{j}^{2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{\to}}\argmax_{j=1,2,\dots,p}\frac{\big\{B^{(j)}(\alpha)+\alpha c^{j}\big\}^{2}}{\sigma_{j}^{2}}=j^{\ast},

\displaystyle T^{(M)}_{n}(\alpha,\hat{k})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{\to}}\frac{\big\{B^{(j^{\ast})}(1)-B^{(j^{\ast})}(\alpha)+(1-\alpha)c^{j^{\ast}}\big\}^{2}}{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\int_{\alpha}^{1}\big\{B^{(j^{\ast})}(r)-\frac{1-r}{1-\alpha}B^{(j^{\ast})}(\alpha)-\frac{r-\alpha}{1-\alpha}B^{(j^{\ast})}(1)\big\}^{2}dr}=U^{\ast}.

Since
j^{\ast}\in\{B^{(k)}(t)\}_{t\in[0,\alpha],k\in\{1,\dots,p\}}
and
U^{\ast}|_{j^{\ast}=j}\in\{B^{(j)}(r)-B^{(j)}(\alpha)\}_{r\in(\alpha,1]}
are independent for any fixed
j
, we have

\displaystyle U^{\ast}|_{j^{\ast}=j}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\frac{\big\{\Gamma_{jj}^{-1/2}B^{(j)}(1)-\Gamma_{jj}^{-1/2}B^{(j)}(\alpha)+\Gamma_{jj}^{-1/2}(1-\alpha)c^{j}\big\}^{2}}{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\int_{\alpha}^{1}\big\{\Gamma_{jj}^{-1/2}B^{(j)}(r)-\Gamma_{jj}^{-1/2}\frac{1-r}{1-\alpha}B^{(j)}(\alpha)-\Gamma_{jj}^{-1/2}\frac{r-\alpha}{1-\alpha}B^{(j)}(1)\big\}^{2}dr}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\frac{\big\{B(1)-B(\alpha)+\Gamma_{jj}^{-1/2}(1-\alpha)c^{j}\big\}^{2}}{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\int_{\alpha}^{1}\big\{B(r)-\frac{1-r}{1-\alpha}B(\alpha)-\frac{r-\alpha}{1-\alpha}B(1)\big\}^{2}dr}

(41)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\frac{\big\{B(1-\alpha)+\Gamma_{jj}^{-1/2}(1-\alpha)c^{j}\big\}^{2}}{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\int_{0}^{1-\alpha}\big\{B(r)-\frac{r}{1-\alpha}B(1-\alpha)\big\}^{2}dr}

(42)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\frac{\big\{B(1)+\sqrt{\frac{1-\alpha}{\Gamma_{jj}}}c^{j}\big\}^{2}}{\int_{0}^{1}\big\{B(r)-rB(1)\big\}^{2}dr}.

(43)

Equation (
41
) follows from the definition of
B^{(j)}(r)
. For Equation (
42
), we used the property
\{B(r+\alpha)-B(\alpha):r\in[0,1-\alpha]\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\{B(r):r\in[0,1-\alpha]\}
and the change of variable property for integration. For Equation (
43
), we used the property
\{B((1-\alpha)r):r\in[0,1]\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\{\sqrt{1-\alpha}B(r):r\in[0,1]\}
. So part (ii).2 of Theorem
1
is proved.

To prove part (ii).3 of Theorem
1
, note that

\displaystyle T^{(M)}_{n}(\alpha,\hat{j})=\frac{[D^{\alpha}(\hat{j})]^{2}+2D^{\alpha}(\hat{j})\sqrt{n-{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}\mu_{n}^{\hat{j}}{+}(n{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor})(\mu_{n}^{\hat{j}})^{2}}{V^{(M)}_{n}(\hat{j})}.

Since
\min_{j=1,2,\dots,p}V^{(M)}_{n}(j)\leq V^{(M)}_{n}(\hat{j})\leq\max_{j=1,2,\dots,p}V^{(M)}_{n}(j)
, we have
\{V^{(M)}_{n}(\hat{j})\}^{-1}=O_{p}(1)
. Since
|D^{\alpha}(\hat{j})|\leq\max_{j=1,2,\dots,p}n^{-1/2}|D^{\alpha}(j)|=O_{p}(1)
and
\sqrt{n}|\mu_{n}^{\hat{j}}|\leq\sqrt{n}||\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}||_{\infty}\to 0
, the limiting distribution of
T^{(M)}_{n}(\alpha,\hat{j})
is the same as the limiting distribution of

\frac{(n-{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor})^{-1}({S}^{\hat{j}}_{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}+1,n}-(n-{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor})\mu_{n}^{\hat{j}})^{2}}{V^{(M)}_{n}(\hat{j})},

which, according to part (i) of this theorem, is
U_{1}
. So part (ii).3 of Theorem
1
is proved.

\qed

C.2
Proof of Theorem
2

Without loss of generality, we assume
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}=\boldsymbol{0}
.
Denote
\mathbf{\mathfrak{D}}_{n}=diag\big\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{\hat{\sigma}_{1}^{2}}},\dots,\frac{1}{\sqrt{\hat{\sigma}_{p}^{2}}}\big\}
,
\mathbf{\mathfrak{D}}=diag\big\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{\sigma_{1}^{2}}},\dots,\frac{1}{\sqrt{\sigma_{p}^{2}}}\big\}
and define

\boldsymbol{\hat{K}}=\mathbf{\mathfrak{D}}\frac{\boldsymbol{S}_{1,{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}}{\sqrt{n}},\,\,\,Q^{(M)}_{n}(\alpha,\boldsymbol{\hat{K}})=\frac{(n-{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor})^{-1}\Big\{\boldsymbol{\hat{K}}^{\top}\big[\mathbf{S}_{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}+1,n}{-}(n{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor})\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}\big]\Big\}^{2}}{V_{n}(\alpha,\boldsymbol{\hat{K}})}

where
V_{n}(\alpha,\boldsymbol{\hat{K}})=(n-{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor})^{-2}\sum_{k={\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}+1}^{n}\Big\{\boldsymbol{\hat{K}}^{\top}\big[\mathbf{S}_{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}+1,k}-\frac{k-{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}{n-{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}\mathbf{S}_{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}+1,n}\big]\Big\}^{2}
.

First we prove part (i) of Theorem
2
. It suffices to show
Q^{(M)}_{n}(\alpha)=Q^{(M)}_{n}(\alpha,\boldsymbol{\hat{K}})(1+o_{p}(1))
and
Q^{(M)}_{n}(\alpha,\boldsymbol{\hat{K}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{\to}}U_{1}
. So we divide the proof into two parts:

(a)
Q^{(M)}_{n}(\alpha)=Q^{(M)}_{n}(\alpha,\boldsymbol{\hat{K}})(1+o_{p}(1))+o_{p}(1)
.

(b)
Q^{(M)}_{n}(\alpha,\boldsymbol{\hat{K}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{\to}}U_{1}
.

Proof of part (a)
: Note that by Slutsky’s Theorem,
||\boldsymbol{\hat{P}}-\boldsymbol{\hat{K}}||=o_{p}(1)
and

\displaystyle(n-{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor})^{-1}\Big|\Big\{\boldsymbol{\hat{P}}^{\top}\big[\mathbf{S}_{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}+1,n}{-}(n{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor})\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}\big]\Big\}^{2}-\Big\{\boldsymbol{\hat{K}}^{\top}\big[\mathbf{S}_{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}+1,n}{-}(n{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor})\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}\big]\Big\}^{2}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle||\boldsymbol{\hat{P}}-\boldsymbol{\hat{K}}||(||\boldsymbol{\hat{P}}||+||\boldsymbol{\hat{K}}||)(n-{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor})^{-1}||\mathbf{S}_{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}+1,n}{-}(n{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor})\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}||^{2}.

(44)

By Assumption
1
,
(n-{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor})^{-1}||\mathbf{S}_{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}+1,n}{-}(n{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor})\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}||^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{\to}}(1-\alpha)^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{1/2}||\mathbf{B}_{p}(1)-\mathbf{B}_{p}(\alpha)||^{2}
, so from Equation (
C.2
) we have

(n-{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor})^{-1}\Big|\Big\{\boldsymbol{\hat{P}}^{\top}\big[\mathbf{S}_{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}+1,n}{-}(n{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor})\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}\big]\Big\}^{2}-\Big\{\boldsymbol{\hat{K}}^{\top}\big[\mathbf{S}_{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}+1,n}{-}(n{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor})\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}\big]\Big\}^{2}\Big|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}0.

Following similar argument, we have
V_{n}(\alpha)-V_{n}(\alpha,\boldsymbol{\hat{K}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}0
and, by the continuous mapping theorem,

V_{n}(\alpha,\boldsymbol{\hat{K}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{\to}}{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\int_{\alpha}^{1}\big\{\boldsymbol{B}_{p}(\alpha)^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{1/2}\mathbf{\mathfrak{D}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{1/2}[\mathbf{B}_{p}(r)-\frac{1-r}{1-\alpha}\mathbf{B}_{p}(\alpha)-\frac{r-\alpha}{1-\alpha}\mathbf{B}_{p}(1)]\big\}^{2}dr},

which is a continuous random variable supported on
[0,\infty)
.
Then we have

Q^{(M)}_{n}(\alpha)=\frac{(n{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor})^{-1}\Big\{\boldsymbol{\hat{K}}^{\top}\big[\mathbf{S}_{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}+1,n}{-}(n{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor})\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}\big]\Big\}^{2}{+}o_{p}(1)}{V_{n}(\alpha,\boldsymbol{\hat{K}}){+}o_{p}(1)}=Q^{(M)}_{n}(\alpha,\boldsymbol{\hat{K}})(1+o_{p}(1)){+}o_{p}(1)

and part (a) is proved.

Proof of part (b)
: Following the same argument as in the proof of Lemma
1
, We know that the functional
F(\cdot):D^{p}[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
defined as

F(\mathbf{x})=\frac{\big\{\mathbf{x}(\alpha)^{\top}\mathbf{\mathfrak{D}}[\mathbf{x}(1)-\mathbf{x}(\alpha)]\big\}^{2}}{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\int_{\alpha}^{1}\big\{\mathbf{x}(\alpha)^{\top}\mathbf{\mathfrak{D}}[\mathbf{x}(r)-\frac{1-r}{1-\alpha}\mathbf{x}(\alpha)-\frac{r-\alpha}{1-\alpha}\mathbf{x}(1)]\big\}^{2}dr}

is continuous a.e. with respect to the probability measure induced by the Brownian motion
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}_{p}(r)
, so by continuous mapping theorem, Assumptions
1
and
2
, we have

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\hat{K}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{\to}}\mathbf{\mathfrak{D}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}_{p}(\alpha)=\boldsymbol{\tilde{K}},

(45)

\displaystyle Q^{(M)}_{n}(\alpha,\boldsymbol{\hat{K}})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{\to}}\frac{\big\{\boldsymbol{\tilde{P}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{1/2}[\mathbf{B}_{p}(1)-\mathbf{B}_{p}(\alpha)]\big\}^{2}}{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\int_{\alpha}^{1}\big\{\boldsymbol{\tilde{P}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{1/2}[\mathbf{B}_{p}(r)-\frac{1-r}{1-\alpha}\mathbf{B}_{p}(\alpha)-\frac{r-\alpha}{1-\alpha}\mathbf{B}_{p}(1)]\big\}^{2}dr}=\tilde{Q}.

(46)

Denote
\tilde{\Gamma}=\sqrt{\boldsymbol{\tilde{K}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{\tilde{K}}}
, by the same conditioning method used in the proof of part (i) of Theorem
1
, the conditional distribution of
\tilde{Q}
given
\boldsymbol{\tilde{K}}
is

\displaystyle\tilde{Q}\Big|\boldsymbol{\tilde{K}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\frac{\big\{\boldsymbol{\tilde{K}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{1/2}[\mathbf{B}_{p}(1)-\mathbf{B}_{p}(\alpha)]\big\}^{2}}{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\int_{\alpha}^{1}\big\{\boldsymbol{\tilde{K}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{1/2}[\mathbf{B}_{p}(r)-\frac{1-r}{1-\alpha}\mathbf{B}_{p}(\alpha)-\frac{r-\alpha}{1-\alpha}\mathbf{B}_{p}(1)]\big\}^{2}dr}\Big|\boldsymbol{\tilde{K}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\frac{\big\{\tilde{\Gamma}[B(1)-B(\alpha)]\big\}^{2}}{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\int_{\alpha}^{1}\big\{\tilde{\Gamma}[B(r)-\frac{1-r}{1-\alpha}B(\alpha)-\frac{r-\alpha}{1-\alpha}B(1)]\big\}^{2}dr}\Big|\boldsymbol{\tilde{K}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\frac{\big\{B(1)-B(\alpha)\big\}^{2}}{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\int_{\alpha}^{1}\big\{B(r)-\frac{1-r}{1-\alpha}B(\alpha)-\frac{r-\alpha}{1-\alpha}B(1)\big\}^{2}dr}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\frac{\big\{B(1)\big\}^{2}}{\int_{0}^{1}\big\{B(r)-rB(1)\big\}^{2}dr}.

So part (i) of Theorem
2
is proved.

To prove part (ii).1 of Theorem
2
, note that the numerator of
Q_{n}(\alpha)
can be written as

\displaystyle\frac{n}{n{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}\Big\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\big[\boldsymbol{S}_{1,{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n}{+}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n}{-}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0})\big]^{\top}\mathbf{\mathfrak{D}}_{n}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\big[\mathbf{S}_{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}+1,n}{-}(n{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor})\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n}{+}(n{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor})(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0})\big]\Big\}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{n}{n{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}\Big\{N_{1}+N_{2}+N_{3}+\frac{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}(n{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor})}{n}(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n}{-}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0})^{\top}\mathbf{\mathfrak{D}}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0})\Big\}^{2},

(47)

where
N_{1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\big[\boldsymbol{S}_{1,{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n}\big]^{\top}\mathbf{\mathfrak{D}}_{n}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\big[\mathbf{S}_{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}+1,n}{-}(n{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor})\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n}\big]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{\to}}\mathbf{B}_{p}(\alpha)^{\top}\mathbf{\mathfrak{D}}[\mathbf{B}_{p}(1)-\mathbf{B}_{p}(\alpha)]
. Since

\displaystyle|N_{2}|

\displaystyle=\Bigg|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\big[\boldsymbol{S}_{1,{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n}\big]^{\top}\mathbf{\mathfrak{D}}_{n}\frac{n{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}{\sqrt{n}}(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n}{-}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0})\Bigg|

\displaystyle\leq||\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\big[\boldsymbol{S}_{1,{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n}\big]||\frac{1}{\min\limits_{j=1,2,\dots,p}\sqrt{\hat{\sigma}_{j}^{2}}}\frac{n{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}{\sqrt{n}}||\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n}{-}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}||,

and
||\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\big[\boldsymbol{S}_{1,{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n}\big]||\frac{1}{\min\limits_{j=1,2,\dots,p}\sqrt{\hat{\sigma}_{j}^{2}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{\to}}||\mathbf{B}_{p}(\alpha)||\frac{1}{\min\limits_{j=1,2,\dots,p}\sqrt{\sigma_{j}^{2}}}
, we have
N_{2}=O_{p}(\sqrt{n}||\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n}{-}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}||)
. For the same reason,
N_{3}=\frac{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}{\sqrt{n}}(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0})^{\top}\mathbf{\mathfrak{D}}_{n}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\big[\mathbf{S}_{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}+1,n}{-}(n{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor})\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n}\big]=O_{p}(\sqrt{n}||\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n}{-}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}||)
. Since
(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n}{-}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0})^{\top}\mathbf{\mathfrak{D}}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n}{-}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0})\geq\frac{1}{\max\limits_{j\in\{1,2,\dots,p\}}\sqrt{\hat{\sigma}_{j}^{2}}}||\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n}{-}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}||^{2}
, the numerator is of the same order as
n^{2}||\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n}{-}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}||^{4}
.

For the denominator of
Q_{n}(\alpha)
, denote
\mathbf{b}_{k}=\mathbf{S}_{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}+1,k}{-}\frac{k-{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}{n-{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}\mathbf{S}_{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}+1,n},k={\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}{+}1,\dots,n
, then we have

\displaystyle\Big\{\boldsymbol{\hat{P}}^{\top}\big[\mathbf{S}_{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}+1,k}-\frac{k-{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}{n-{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}\mathbf{S}_{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}+1,n}\big]\Big\}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\big[\boldsymbol{S}_{1,{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n}\big]^{\top}\mathbf{\mathfrak{D}}_{n}\mathbf{b}_{k}{+}\frac{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}{\sqrt{n}}(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n}{-}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0})^{\top}\mathbf{\mathfrak{D}}_{n}\mathbf{b}_{k}\Big\}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\Big\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\big[\boldsymbol{S}_{1,{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n}\big]^{\top}\mathbf{\mathfrak{D}}_{n}\mathbf{b}_{k}\Big\}^{2}{+}2\frac{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}^{2}}{n}||\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n}{-}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}||^{2}\frac{1}{\min\limits_{j=1,2,\dots,p}\hat{\sigma}_{j}^{2}}||\mathbf{b}_{k}||^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2||\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\big[\boldsymbol{S}_{1,{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n}\big]||^{2}\frac{1}{\min\limits_{j=1,2,\dots,p}\hat{\sigma}_{j}^{2}}||\mathbf{b}_{k}||^{2}{+}2\frac{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}^{2}}{n}||\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n}{-}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}||^{2}\frac{1}{\min\limits_{j=1,2,\dots,p}\hat{\sigma}_{j}^{2}}||\mathbf{b}_{k}||^{2}.

(48)

Since
(n{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor})^{-2}\sum_{k={\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}{+}1}^{n}||\mathbf{b}_{k}||^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{\to}}(1{-}\alpha)^{-2}\int_{\alpha}^{1}||\mathbf{B}_{p}(r){-}\mathbf{B}_{p}(\alpha){-}\frac{r{-}\alpha}{1{-}\alpha}(\mathbf{B}_{p}(1){-}\mathbf{B}_{p}(\alpha))||^{2}dr
, the denominator of
Q_{n}(\alpha)
is of the same order as
n||\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n}{-}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}||^{2}
and part (ii).1 of Theorem
2
is proved.

To prove part (ii).2 of Theorem
2
, following the same idea as in the proof of part (i), we only need to show

\boldsymbol{\hat{k}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{\to}}\mathbf{\mathfrak{D}}\big[\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}_{p}(\alpha)+\alpha\mathbf{c}\big]=\boldsymbol{P}^{\ast}\mbox{ and }Q^{(M)}_{n}(\alpha,\boldsymbol{\hat{K}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{\to}}U^{\ast\ast}.

By continuous mapping theorem,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\hat{K}}=\mathbf{\mathfrak{D}}\frac{\boldsymbol{S}_{1,{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}}{\sqrt{n}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{\to}}\mathbf{\mathfrak{D}}\big[\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{1/2}\mathbf{B}_{p}(\alpha)+\alpha\mathbf{c}\big]=\boldsymbol{P}^{\ast},

\displaystyle Q^{(M)}_{n}(\alpha,\boldsymbol{\hat{K}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{\to}}\frac{\big\{(\boldsymbol{P}^{\ast})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{1/2}(\mathbf{B}_{p}(1){-}\mathbf{B}_{p}(\alpha)){+}(1{-}\alpha)(\boldsymbol{P}^{\ast})^{\top}\mathbf{c}\big\}^{2}}{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\int_{\alpha}^{1}\big\{(\boldsymbol{{P}}^{\ast})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{1/2}[\mathbf{B}_{p}(r){-}\mathbf{B}_{p}(\alpha){-}\frac{r-\alpha}{1-\alpha}(\mathbf{B}_{p}(1)-\mathbf{B}_{p}(\alpha))]\big\}^{2}dr}=U^{\ast\ast}.

Since conditioning on
\boldsymbol{P}^{\ast}
,
\{(\boldsymbol{P}^{\ast})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{1/2}[\mathbf{B}_{p}(r){-}\mathbf{B}_{p}(\alpha)]\}_{r\in(\alpha,1]}
is a Gaussian process with covariance function
\cov\big\{(\boldsymbol{P}^{\ast})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{1/2}[\mathbf{B}_{p}(r_{1}){-}\mathbf{B}_{p}(\alpha)],(\boldsymbol{P}^{\ast})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{1/2}[\mathbf{B}_{p}(r_{2}){-}\mathbf{B}_{p}(\alpha)]\big\}=\min\{r_{1}-\alpha,r_{2}-\alpha\}(\boldsymbol{P}^{\ast})^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{P}^{\ast}
, part (ii).2 of Theorem
2
can be proved in a similar way as in the proof for part (ii).2 and (ii).3 of Theorem
1
.

To prove part (ii).3 of Theorem
2
, note that
\sqrt{n}||\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n}{-}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}||\to 0
implies
N_{2},N_{3}
and
\frac{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}(n{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor})}{n}||\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n}{-}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}||^{2}
from Equation (
47
) are of order
o_{p}(1)
. Also from Equation (
48
), we have

2\frac{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}^{2}}{n}||\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n}{-}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}||^{2}\frac{1}{\min\limits_{j=1,2,\dots,p}\hat{\sigma}_{j}^{2}}\frac{1}{(n{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor})^{2}}\sum_{k={\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}{{+}}1}^{n}||\mathbf{b}_{k}||^{2}=o_{p}(1),

so
Q^{(M)}_{n}(\alpha)
will have the same limiting distribution as under the null hypothesis.

\qed

C.3
Proof of Proposition
1

From Theorem 3.1 in
Mies and
Steland 2023
, we have

\displaystyle\Big(E\big\{\max\limits_{k\leq n}||\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=1}^{k}(\mathbf{X}^{\prime}_{nt}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n})-W_{n}(\frac{k}{n})||^{2}\big\}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\Theta_{n}\sqrt{\log(n)}(\frac{p_{n}}{n})^{\xi}.

(49)

For
k=1,2,\dots,n
, define iid random variables
Q_{nk}=\sup\limits_{r\in[0,1]}||W^{(k)}_{n}(r)||^{2}=\sup\limits_{r\in[0,1]}||\Gamma_{n}^{1/2}B^{(k)}_{p_{n}}(r)||^{2}
, where
B^{(k)}_{p_{n}}(r)=(B^{(k)}_{p_{n},1}(r),B^{(k)}_{p_{n},2}(r),\dots,B^{(k)}_{p_{n},p_{n}}(r))^{\top}
are independent standard
p_{n}
-dimensional Brownian motions, then we have

\displaystyle E\big\{\sup\limits_{r\in[0,1]}||W_{n}(r)-W_{n}(\frac{{\lfloor nr\rfloor}}{n})||^{2}\big\}=\frac{1}{n}E\big\{\max\limits_{k\leq n}Q_{nk}\big\}.

Note that
||W_{n}^{(k)}(r)||^{2}=B^{(k)}_{p_{n}}(r)^{\top}\Gamma_{n}B^{(k)}_{p_{n}}(r)\leq tr(\Gamma_{n})||B^{(k)}_{p_{n}}(r)||^{2}
, so we have

\displaystyle E\big\{\max\limits_{k\leq n}Q_{nk}\big\}

\displaystyle\leq tr(\Gamma_{n})E\big\{\max\limits_{k\leq n}\sup\limits_{r\in[0,1]}||B^{(k)}_{p_{n}}(r)||^{2}\big\}

\displaystyle\leq tr(\Gamma_{n})\sum_{j=1}^{p_{n}}E\big\{\max\limits_{k\leq n}\sup\limits_{r\in[0,1]}|B^{(k)}_{p_{n},j}(r)|^{2}\big\}

\displaystyle=p_{n}tr(\Gamma_{n})E\big\{\max\limits_{k\leq n}\sup\limits_{r\in[0,1]}|B^{(k)}(r)|^{2}\big\},

where
B^{(k)}(r)
are independent standard
1
-dimensional Brownian motions. By Doob’s inequality in
L^{p}
, we know
\sup\limits_{r\in[0,1]}|B(r)|^{2}
has all positive order moments. It then follows from Corollary 1.1 in
Correa and
Romero 2021
that

\displaystyle E\big\{\max\limits_{k\leq n}\sup\limits_{r\in[0,1]}|B^{(k)}(r)|^{2}\big\}=o(n^{\epsilon})\mbox{ for any small positive }\epsilon.

From this and the fact that
tr(\Gamma_{n})\leq C\Theta_{n}^{2}
for some fixed constant
C
(see Proposition 5.4 in
Mies and
Steland 2023
), we can derive

\displaystyle E\big\{\sup\limits_{r\in[0,1]}||W_{n}(r)-W_{n}(\frac{{\lfloor nr\rfloor}}{n})||^{2}\big\}\leq C\frac{p_{n}\Theta_{n}^{2}}{n^{1-\epsilon}}\mbox{ for any small positive }\epsilon,

(50)

Combining Equations (
49
) and (
50
), the proposition is proved.
\qed

C.4
Proof of Theorem
3

Denote

\displaystyle\hat{j}^{(W)}_{n}

\displaystyle=\argmax_{j=1,2,\dots,p_{n}}\frac{[{W}_{nj}(\alpha)]^{2}}{\sigma_{nj}^{2}}

\displaystyle T^{(W)}_{n}(\alpha,j)

\displaystyle=\frac{(1-\alpha)[{W}_{nj}(1)-{W}_{nj}(\alpha)]^{2}}{\int_{\alpha}^{1}[W_{nj}(s)-W_{nj}(\alpha)-\frac{s-\alpha}{1-\alpha}(W_{nj}(1)-W_{nj}(\alpha))]^{2}ds}.

Let
\tilde{T}^{(D)}_{n}(\alpha,\tilde{j}_{n})
denote the SS-SN statistic calculated using
\{\mathbf{X}^{\prime}_{nt}\}_{t=1}^{n}
. If we can show
\tilde{T}^{(D)}_{n}(\alpha,\tilde{j}_{n})-T^{(W)}_{n}(\alpha,\hat{j}^{(W)}_{n})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}0
, then since
T^{(W)}_{n}(\alpha,\hat{j}^{(W)}_{n})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}U_{1}
, by Slutsky’s Theorem we have
\tilde{T}^{(D)}_{n}(\alpha,\tilde{j}_{n})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{\to}}U_{1}
. Note that
\tilde{T}^{(D)}_{n}(\alpha,\tilde{j}_{n})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}T^{(D)}_{n}(\alpha,\hat{j}_{n})
, so we have
T^{(D)}_{n}(\alpha,\hat{j}_{n})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{\to}}U_{1}
. So for notation simplicity, without loss of generality, we treat
\{\mathbf{X}_{nt}\}_{t=1}^{n}
as
\{\mathbf{X}^{\prime}_{nt}\}_{t=1}^{n}
and assume the former is defined in the same probability space as
\mathbf{W}_{n}(r)
.

If we can show
\hat{j}_{n}-\hat{j}^{(W)}_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}0
and
\max\limits_{j\leq p_{n}}\Big|T^{(D)}_{n}(\alpha,j)-T^{(W)}_{n}(\alpha,j)\Big|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}0
, then the conclusion follows since for any
\epsilon>0
,

\displaystyle P(|T^{(D)}_{n}(\alpha,\hat{j}_{n})-T^{(W)}_{n}(\alpha,\hat{j}^{(W)}_{n})|>\epsilon)\leq P(\hat{j}_{n}\neq\hat{j}^{(W)}_{n})+P(\max\limits_{j\leq p_{n}}\Big|T^{(D)}_{n}(\alpha,j)-T^{(W)}_{n}(\alpha,j)\Big|>\epsilon).

On the space of cadlag functions
D[0,1]
, define
\chi_{ni}(r)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=1}^{{\lfloor nr\rfloor}}X^{i}_{nt}
,
\boldsymbol{\chi}_{n}(r)=(\chi_{n1}(r),\dots,\chi_{np_{n}}(r))^{\top}
,

F(x)=\frac{(x(1))^{2}}{\int_{0}^{1}(x(s)-sx(1))^{2}ds},\mbox{ }F_{\chi}(i)=\frac{(\chi_{ni}(1))^{2}}{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}(X_{nk}^{i}-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}X_{nj}^{i})^{2}},\mbox{ }F_{W}(i)=\frac{(W_{ni}(1))^{2}}{\sigma_{ni}^{2}},

and let
j_{\chi_{n}}=\argmax\limits_{i\leq p_{n}}F_{\chi}(i)
,
j_{W_{n}}=\argmax\limits_{i\leq p_{n}}F_{W}(i)
. By the translation invariance and scaling properties of Brownian motion (see Chapter 7.1 in
Durrett 2019
), it suffices to show
j_{\chi_{n}}-j_{W_{n}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}0
and
\max\limits_{i\leq p_{n}}|F(\chi_{ni})-F(W_{ni})|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}0
, the proof of which is divided into three parts:

(a) Under Assumption
3
and assume
p_{n}\asymp n^{\psi}
for some
0<\psi<\frac{\xi}{\xi+\frac{1}{2}}
, we have
\max\limits_{i\leq p_{n}}|F(\chi_{ni})-F(W_{ni})|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}0
.

(b) Under Assumption
3
and
5
, we have
j_{\chi_{n}}-j_{W_{n}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}0
.

(c) Under Assumption
3
and
4
, we have
j_{\chi_{n}}-j_{W_{n}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}0
.

Proof of part (a)
: For any
i=1,2,\dots,p_{n}
, denote
B_{ni}=W_{ni}/\sqrt{\gamma_{ni}}
, which is a standard Brownian motion. Let
b_{n}=\Theta_{n}^{2}{\log(n)}(\frac{p_{n}}{n})^{2\xi}
be the dominant term on the right hand side of Equation (
10
) and let
a_{n}=\frac{\Theta_{n}^{2}}{\sqrt{n}}
, then we have
b_{n}=o(p_{n}^{-(\frac{2\xi}{\psi}-2\xi-1-\eta)})
for any small
\eta>0
and
a_{n}=O(p_{n}^{1-\frac{1}{2\psi}})
. Note that
\xi
is always smaller than
\frac{1}{6}
and, as a consequence,
\psi
is always smaller than
\frac{1}{4}
and
a_{n}=o(p_{n}^{-1})
. Denote
D_{ni}=\int_{0}^{1}(W_{ni}(r)-rW_{ni}(1))^{2}dr
, then for any
\epsilon>0
we have

\displaystyle P(\max\limits_{i\leq p_{n}}|F(\chi_{ni}){-}F(W_{ni})|\geq\epsilon)

\displaystyle\leq P(\min\limits_{i\leq p_{n}}D_{ni}\leq\lambda_{n}){+}P(\min\limits_{i\leq p_{n}}D_{ni}>\lambda_{n};\max\limits_{i\leq p_{n}}|F(\chi_{ni}){-}F(W_{ni})|\geq\epsilon).

(51)

Set
\lambda_{n}=\frac{\gamma_{min}}{10\log p_{n}}
. Then it follows from Lemma
D.12
that

\displaystyle P(\min\limits_{i\leq p_{n}}D_{ni}\leq\lambda_{n})

\displaystyle\leq P(\gamma_{min}\min\limits_{i\leq p_{n}}\int_{0}^{1}(B_{ni}(r)-rB_{ni}(1))^{2}dr\leq\lambda_{n})

\displaystyle\leq p_{n}P(\int_{0}^{1}(B(r)-rB(1))^{2}dr\leq\frac{1}{10\log p_{n}})\to 0.

(52)

By the uniform upper and lower bound of
\gamma_{ni}
, we have that given
\min\limits_{i\leq p_{n}}D_{ni}>\lambda_{n}
,

\displaystyle F(\chi_{ni})=\frac{(\chi_{ni}(1))^{2}}{\int_{0}^{1}(\chi_{ni}(r)-r\chi_{ni}(1))^{2}dr}=\frac{(W_{ni}(1))^{2}+|W_{ni}(1)|O_{p}(\sqrt{b_{n}})}{D_{ni}+\sqrt{D_{ni}}O_{p}(\sqrt{b_{n}})},

where
O_{p}(\sqrt{b_{n}})
is uniform in
i=1,2,\dots,p_{n}
. It follows that

\displaystyle|F(\chi_{ni})-F(W_{ni})|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big|F(W_{ni})\Big\{\frac{D_{ni}}{D_{ni}+\sqrt{D_{ni}}O_{p}(\sqrt{b_{n}})}-1\Big\}+\frac{|W_{ni}(1)|O_{p}(\sqrt{b_{n}})}{D_{ni}+\sqrt{D_{ni}}O_{p}(\sqrt{b_{n}})}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle F(W_{ni})\frac{\sqrt{D_{ni}}O_{p}(\sqrt{b_{n}})}{D_{ni}+\sqrt{D_{ni}}O_{p}(\sqrt{b_{n}})}+\frac{|W_{ni}(1)|O_{p}(\sqrt{b_{n}})}{D_{ni}+\sqrt{D_{ni}}O_{p}(\sqrt{b_{n}})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{|W_{ni}(1)|^{2}O_{p}(\sqrt{b_{n}})}{(D_{ni})^{3/2}+D_{ni}O_{p}(\sqrt{b_{n}})}+\frac{|W_{ni}(1)|O_{p}(\sqrt{b_{n}})}{D_{ni}+\sqrt{D_{ni}}O_{p}(\sqrt{b_{n}})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{|W_{ni}(1)|^{2}O_{p}(\frac{\sqrt{b_{n}}}{\lambda_{n}^{3/2}})}{1+O_{p}(\sqrt{\frac{b_{n}}{\lambda_{n}}})}+\frac{|W_{ni}(1)|O_{p}(\frac{\sqrt{b_{n}}}{\lambda_{n}})}{1+O_{p}(\sqrt{\frac{b_{n}}{\lambda_{n}}})}.

(53)

Since
P(\max\limits_{i\leq p_{n}}|W_{ni}(1)|>\epsilon)\leq P(\max\limits_{i\leq p_{n}}|B_{ni}(1)|>\epsilon/\sqrt{\gamma_{max}})
and
|B_{ni}(1)|
has all orders of moments, by Lemma
D.13
we have
\max\limits_{i\leq p_{n}}|W_{ni}(1)|=o_{p}(p_{n}^{\delta})
for any small
\delta>0
and the same result also holds for
\max\limits_{i\leq p_{n}}|W_{ni}(1)|^{2}
. By Equation (
53
) we have
\max\limits_{i\leq p_{n}}|F(\chi_{ni})-F(W_{ni})|=o_{p}(b_{n}^{\frac{1}{2}-\eta})
for any small
\eta>0
given
\min\limits_{i\leq p_{n}}D_{ni}>\lambda_{n}
. Combining this result with Equation (
C.4
), we have the right hand side of Equation (
51
) converges to 0 as
n\to\infty
. So part (a) is proved.

Proof of part (b)
:
From Lemma
D.17
we have
\max\limits_{i\leq p_{n}}|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}(X_{nk}^{i}-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}X_{nj}^{i})^{2}-\sigma_{ni}^{2}|=O_{p}(a_{n})
and

\displaystyle|F_{\chi}(i)-F_{W}(i)|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(W_{ni}(1))^{2}}{\sigma_{ni}^{2}}\Big[\frac{\sigma_{ni}^{2}}{\sigma_{ni}^{2}+O_{p}(a_{n})}-1\Big]+\frac{|W_{ni}(1)|O_{P}(\sqrt{b_{n}})}{\sigma_{ni}^{2}+O_{p}(a_{n})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{\max_{i\leq p_{n}}(W_{ni}(1))^{2}}{\sigma_{min}^{2}}\Big[\frac{O_{p}(a_{n})}{\sigma_{min}^{2}+O_{p}(a_{n})}\Big]+\frac{\max_{i\leq p_{n}}|W_{ni}(1)|O_{P}(\sqrt{b_{n}})}{\sigma_{min}^{2}+O_{p}(a_{n})},

so we can find a small positive real number
\epsilon_{0}
such that
\max\limits_{i\leq p_{n}}p_{n}^{\epsilon_{0}}|F_{\chi}(i)-F_{W}(i)|=o_{p}(1)
. For
i=1,2,\dots,p_{n}
denote
a_{ni}=\frac{\gamma_{ni}}{\sigma_{ni}^{2}}
,
V_{ni}=F_{W}(i)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}a_{ni}Z_{i}
where
Z_{i}
are i.i.d chi-square distributed with one degree of freedom. Note that under Assumption
3
, there exist
0<\underline{a}<\bar{a}
such that
\underline{a}\leq a_{ni}\leq\bar{a}
for all
i=1,2,\dots,p_{n}
. Let
K_{ni}(\cdot)
and
k_{ni}(\cdot)
be the pdf and cdf of
V_{ni}
and
V_{(1)}\leq V_{(2)}\leq\cdots\leq V_{(p_{n})}
be the order statistics of
\{V_{1},V_{2}\dots,V_{p_{n}}\}
.

Note that
j_{\chi_{n}}\neq j_{W_{n}}
implies
V_{(p_{n})}-V_{(p_{n}-1)}\leq 2\max\limits_{i\leq p_{n}}|F_{\chi}(i)-F_{W}(i)|
, so it suffices to show
p_{n}^{\epsilon_{0}}(V_{(p_{n})}-V_{(p_{n}-1)})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}\infty
. Since the joint pdf of
(V_{(p_{n})},V_{(p_{n}-1)})^{\top}
at
(x_{1},x_{2})^{\top}
is

\displaystyle f_{V}(x_{1},x_{2})=\sum\limits_{(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{p_{n}})}k_{ni_{1}}(x_{1})k_{ni_{2}}(x_{2})\prod_{j=3}^{p_{n}}K_{ni_{j}}(x_{2}),

where the summation
\sum\limits_{(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{p_{n}})}
is over all permutations of
\{1,2,\dots,p_{n}\}
, the joint pdf of
(V_{(p_{n})}-V_{(p_{n}-1)},V_{(p_{n}-1)})^{\top}
at
(y_{1},y_{2})^{\top}
is

\displaystyle f_{S}(y_{1},y_{2})=\sum\limits_{(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{p_{n}})}k_{ni_{1}}(y_{1}+y_{2})k_{ni_{2}}(y_{2})\prod_{j=3}^{p_{n}}K_{ni_{j}}(y_{2})

and the pdf of
V_{(p_{n})}-V_{(p_{n}-1)}
at
y_{1}
is

\displaystyle f_{A}(y_{1})=\sum\limits_{(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{p_{n}})}\int_{0}^{\infty}k_{ni_{1}}(y_{1}+y_{2})k_{ni_{2}}(y_{2})\prod_{j=3}^{p_{n}}K_{ni_{j}}(y_{2})dy_{2}.

Let
Y_{i}(a)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i.i.d}}{{\sim}}Gamma(1/2,2a)
, or equivalently
Y_{i}(a)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}aZ_{i}
, with scale parameter
{2a}
and denote its pdf and cdf as
f_{a}(\cdot)
and
F_{a}(\cdot)
. Let
Y_{(1)}(a)\leq Y_{(2)}(a)\leq\cdots\leq Y_{(p_{n})}(a)
be the order statistics of
\{Y_{1}(a),Y_{2}(a)\dots,Y_{p_{n}}(a)\}
. For fixed
y\in(0,\infty)
, it is well known that
F_{a}(y)
is a decreasing function of
a
,
f_{a}(y)
is a decreasing function of
a
for
a>y
and an increasing function of
a
for
a<y
. Fix
(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{p_{n}})^{\top}
as any permutation of
\{1,2,\dots,p_{n}\}
and
y_{1}\in(0,\frac{\underline{a}}{2})
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}k_{ni_{1}}(y_{1}+y_{2})k_{ni_{2}}(y_{2})\prod_{j=3}^{p_{n}}K_{ni_{j}}(y_{2})dy_{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\underline{a}/2}f_{\underline{a}}(y_{1}{+}y_{2})f_{\underline{a}}(y_{2})[F_{\underline{a}}(y_{2})]^{p_{n}{-}2}dy_{2}{+}\sup_{b\in[\underline{a},\bar{a}],y_{1}\in(0,\frac{\underline{a}}{2})}\int_{\underline{a}/2}^{\infty}f_{b}(y_{1}{+}y_{2})f_{b}(y_{2})[F_{\underline{a}}(y_{2})]^{p_{n}{-}2}dy_{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\int_{0}^{\infty}f_{\underline{a}}(y_{1}{+}y_{2})f_{\underline{a}}(y_{2})[F_{\underline{a}}(y_{2})]^{p_{n}{-}2}dy_{2},

for some constant
C>1
which does not depend on
y_{1}
. Summing over all permutations of
\{1,2,\dots,p_{n}\}
, the above inequality implies the density of
V_{(p_{n})}{-}V_{(p_{n}{-}1)}
at
y_{1}\in(0,\frac{\underline{a}}{2})
is less than
C
times the density of
Y_{(p_{n})}(\underline{a}){-}Y_{(p_{n}{-}1)}(\underline{a})
at
y_{1}
. By Corollary 4.2.11 in
Embrechts et al. 2013
, we have
\frac{1}{2\underline{a}}[Y_{(p_{n})}(\underline{a}){-}Y_{(p_{n}{-}1)}(\underline{a})]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{\to}}\tilde{Y}\sim exp(1)
. For any
\epsilon>0
, fix
0<\tilde{c}<\frac{\underline{a}}{2}
such that
P(\tilde{Y}<\frac{\tilde{c}}{2\underline{a}})<\frac{\epsilon}{C}
, then we have

\displaystyle P(V_{(p_{n})}{-}V_{(p_{n}{-}1)}<\tilde{c})\leq CP(Y_{(p_{n})}(\underline{a}){-}Y_{(p_{n}{-}1)}(\underline{a})<\tilde{c})\to CP(\tilde{Y}<\frac{\tilde{c}}{2\underline{a}})<\epsilon,

(54)

which implies
(V_{(p_{n})}{-}V_{(p_{n}{-}1)})^{-1}=O_{p}(1)
and part (b) is proved.

Proof of part (c)
: Note that under Assumption
4
,
\frac{2\xi}{\psi}-2\xi-1>8
. It follows from Equation (
53
) that we can find some small number
\epsilon_{0}>0
such that for any
\epsilon>0
,
\max\limits_{i\leq p_{n}}|F(\chi_{ni})-F(W_{ni})|=o_{p}(p_{n}^{-(4+\epsilon_{0})})
. From the proof of part (b), we know that
j_{\chi_{n}}\neq j_{W_{n}}
implies that
V_{(p_{n})}-V_{(p_{n}-1)}\leq 2\max\limits_{i\leq p_{n}}|F_{\chi}(i)-F_{W}(i)|
. If for some fixed
C_{1}>0
, we can show that

\displaystyle P(V_{(p_{n})}-V_{(p_{n}-1)}\leq C_{1}p_{n}^{-(4+\epsilon_{0})})\to 0,

(55)

then

\displaystyle P(\frac{V_{(p_{n})}{-}V_{(p_{n}-1)}}{\max\limits_{i\leq p_{n}}|F(\chi_{ni}){-}F(W_{ni})|}\leq 2)\leq

\displaystyle P(\frac{C_{1}p_{n}^{-(4+\epsilon_{0})}}{\max\limits_{i\leq p_{n}}|F(\chi_{ni}){-}F(W_{ni})|}\leq 2){+}P(V_{(p_{n})}{-}V_{(p_{n}-1)}\leq C_{1}p_{n}^{-(4+\epsilon_{0})})

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle 0,

and the problem is proved.

From Lemma
D.15
and the uniform boundedness of
\rho_{nij},\gamma_{ni}
and
\sigma_{ni}^{2}
, we know that for
0<C<1
,
P(|V_{i}-V_{j}|<C)\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi(1-\rho_{nij}^{2})(1-|\rho_{nij}|)}}(\sqrt{\frac{\sigma^{2}_{ni}}{\gamma_{ni}}}+\sqrt{\frac{\sigma^{2}_{nj}}{\gamma_{nj}}})\sqrt{C}\leq\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi(1-\bar{\rho}^{2})(1-\bar{\rho})}}\sqrt{\frac{\sigma^{2}_{max}}{\gamma_{min}}}\sqrt{C}
. For large enough
n
,
C_{1}p_{n}^{-(4+\epsilon_{0})}\in(0,1)
, so we can derive that

\displaystyle P(V_{(p_{n})}-V_{(p_{n}-1)}\leq C_{1}p_{n}^{-(4+\epsilon_{0})})

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{i\neq j}P(|V_{i}-V_{j}|<C_{1}p_{n}^{-(4+\epsilon_{0})})

\displaystyle\leq p_{n}(p_{n}-1)\frac{8}{4\sqrt{\pi(1-\bar{\rho}^{2})(1-\bar{\rho})}}\sqrt{\frac{\sigma^{2}_{max}}{\gamma_{min}}}\sqrt{C_{1}}p_{n}^{-2-\frac{\epsilon_{0}}{2}}

\displaystyle\to 0,

and part (c) is proved.
\qed

C.5
Proof of Theorem
4

Following the same argument as at the beginning of the proof of Theorem
3
, we treat
\{\mathbf{X}_{nt}\}_{t=1}^{n}
as
\{\mathbf{X}^{\prime}_{nt}\}_{t=1}^{n}
and assume the former is defined in the same probability space as
\mathbf{W}_{n}(r)
.

Note that for any
n=1,2,\dots
, there exist positive integer
j_{n}\leq p_{n}
such that
\frac{\sqrt{n}|{\mu}_{n}^{j_{n}}|}{p_{n}^{\kappa}}\to\infty
. Denote
A_{n}^{\alpha}(j)={S}^{nj}_{1,{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}-{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}\mu_{n}^{j}
, then by the definition of
\hat{j}_{n}
we have

\displaystyle\frac{2{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}^{-1}\max\limits_{j=1,2,\dots,p_{n}}[A_{n}^{\alpha}(j)]^{2}+2{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}[\mu_{n}^{\hat{j}_{n}}]^{2}}{\min\limits_{j=1,2,\dots,p_{n}}\hat{\sigma}_{nj}^{2}}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}^{-1}[{S}^{n\hat{j}_{n}}_{1,{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}]^{2}}{\hat{\sigma}_{n\hat{j}_{n}}^{2}}\geq\frac{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}^{-1}[{S}^{nj_{n}}_{1,{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}]^{2}}{\hat{\sigma}_{nj_{n}}^{2}}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}^{-1}\min\limits_{j=1,2,\dots,p_{n}}[A_{n}^{\alpha}(j)]^{2}-\frac{2}{\sqrt{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}}\max\limits_{j=1,2,\dots,p_{n}}|A_{n}^{\alpha}(j)|\sqrt{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}|\mu_{n}^{j_{n}}|+{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}[\mu_{n}^{j_{n}}]^{2}}{\max\limits_{j=1,2,\dots,p_{n}}\hat{\sigma}_{nj}^{2}}.

(56)

Note that

\displaystyle n^{-1}\max\limits_{j=1,2,\dots,p_{n}}[A_{n}^{\alpha}(j)]^{2}\leq 2\max\limits_{j=1,2,\dots,p_{n}}|W_{nj}(\alpha)|^{2}+2\sup\limits_{r\in[0,1]}||\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=1}^{{\lfloor nr\rfloor}}(\mathbf{X}^{\prime}_{nt}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n})-W_{n}(r)||^{2},

(57)

and by Lemma
D.13
we have
\max\limits_{j=1,2,\dots,p_{n}}|W_{nj}(\alpha)|^{2}=o_{p}(p_{n}^{\epsilon})
for any small
\epsilon>0
, so we have
{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}^{-1}\max\limits_{j=1,2,\dots,p_{n}}[A_{n}^{\alpha}(j)]^{2}=o_{p}(p_{n}^{\epsilon})
and
\frac{1}{\sqrt{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}}\max\limits_{j=1,2,\dots,p_{n}}|A_{n}^{\alpha}(j)|=o_{p}(p_{n}^{\frac{\epsilon}{2}})
for any small
\epsilon>0
. Multiplying
\max\limits_{j=1,2,\dots,p_{n}}\hat{\sigma}_{nj}^{2}
on both sides of Inequality (
56
), we get

\displaystyle\frac{\max\limits_{j=1,2,\dots,p_{n}}\hat{\sigma}_{nj}^{2}}{\min\limits_{j=1,2,\dots,p_{n}}\hat{\sigma}_{nj}^{2}}\Big\{o_{p}(p_{n}^{\epsilon})+{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}[\mu_{n}^{\hat{j}_{n}}]^{2}\Big\}\geq O_{p}(1)-o_{p}(p_{n}^{\frac{\epsilon}{2}})\sqrt{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}|\mu_{n}^{j_{n}}|+{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}[\mu_{n}^{j_{n}}]^{2}.

(58)

Since
\sigma_{nj}^{2}
are uniformly bounded and from Lemma
D.17
,
\max\limits_{j\leq p_{n}}|\hat{\sigma}_{nj}^{2}-\sigma_{nj}^{2}|=O_{p}(a_{n})=o_{p}(1)
, it is easy to see that
\frac{\max\limits_{j=1,2,\dots,p_{n}}\hat{\sigma}_{nj}^{2}}{\min\limits_{j=1,2,\dots,p_{n}}\hat{\sigma}_{nj}^{2}}=\frac{\max\limits_{j=1,2,\dots,p_{n}}\sigma_{nj}^{2}}{\min\limits_{j=1,2,\dots,p_{n}}\sigma_{nj}^{2}}(1+o_{p}(1))
. So for any small
\epsilon\in(0,2\kappa)
, Inequality (
58
) can be written as

\displaystyle O_{p}(1)\{o_{p}(p_{n}^{\epsilon})+{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}[\mu_{n}^{\hat{j}_{n}}]^{2}}\}\geq{O_{p}(1)+{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}[\mu_{n}^{j_{n}}]^{2}}(1+o_{p}(1))}.

(59)

Dividing both sides of Inequality (
59
) by
p_{n}^{2\kappa}
, it is easy to see
\frac{\sqrt{n}|\mu_{n}^{\hat{j}_{n}}|}{p_{n}^{\kappa}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}\infty
. Denote
A_{n}(j)={S}^{nj}_{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}+1,n}{-}(n{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor})\mu_{n}^{j}
, we have

\displaystyle T^{(D)}_{n}(\alpha,\hat{j}_{n})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(n-{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor})^{-1}[{S}^{n\hat{j}_{n}}_{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}+1,n}]^{2}}{V^{(D)}_{n}(\hat{j}_{n})}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{\frac{1}{n{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}\min\limits_{j=1,2,\dots,p_{n}}[A_{n}(j)]^{2}{-}\frac{2}{\sqrt{n{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}}\max\limits_{j=1,2,\dots,p_{n}}|A_{n}(j)|\sqrt{n{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}}|\mu_{n}^{\hat{j}_{n}}|{+}(n{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor})[\mu_{n}^{\hat{j}_{n}}]^{2}}{\max\limits_{j=1,2,\dots,p_{n}}V^{(D)}_{n}(j)}.

(60)

Following the same argument as in Equation (
57
), it is easy to see that
\max\limits_{j=1,2,\dots,p_{n}}V_{n}^{(D)}(j)=o_{p}(p_{n}^{\epsilon})
for any small
\epsilon>0
. Dividing both numerator and denominator on the right hand side of Inequality (
60
) by
p_{n}^{2\kappa}
, we get

\displaystyle T^{(D)}_{n}(\alpha,\hat{j}_{n})\geq\frac{o_{p}(1){-}o_{p}(1)\frac{\sqrt{n}|\mu_{n}^{\hat{j}_{n}}|}{p_{n}^{\kappa}}{+}\frac{(n{-}{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor})[\mu_{n}^{\hat{j}_{n}}]^{2}}{p_{n}^{2\kappa}}}{o_{p}(1)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}\infty,

and the theorem is proved.

\qed

C.6
Proof of Theorem
5

Following the same argument as at the beginning of the proof of Theorem
3
, we treat
\{\mathbf{X}_{nt}\}_{t=1}^{n}
as
\{\mathbf{X}^{\prime}_{nt}\}_{t=1}^{n}
and assume the former is defined in the same probability space as
\mathbf{W}_{n}(r)
.

Denote
\boldsymbol{{P}}_{n}=(\frac{W_{n1}(\alpha)}{\sigma_{n1}},\dots,\frac{W_{np_{n}}(\alpha)}{\sigma_{np_{n}}})^{\top}
. We first prove a bound on
||\boldsymbol{\hat{P}}_{n}-\boldsymbol{P}_{n}||^{2}
. Recall that
a_{n}=\frac{\Theta_{n}^{2}}{\sqrt{n}}=o(1)
and from Lemma
D.17
we have
\max\limits_{j\leq p_{n}}|\hat{\sigma}_{nj}^{2}-\sigma_{nj}^{2}|=O_{p}(a_{n})
. For
i=1,2,\dots,p_{n}

\displaystyle|\hat{\sigma}_{ni}-\sigma_{ni}|=\frac{|\hat{\sigma}_{ni}^{2}-\sigma_{ni}^{2}|}{|\hat{\sigma}_{ni}+\sigma_{ni}|}\leq\frac{O_{p}(a_{n})}{\sqrt{2\sigma_{min}^{2}+O_{p}(a_{n})}}=O_{p}(a_{n}),

(61)

and

\displaystyle||\boldsymbol{\hat{P}}_{n}-\boldsymbol{P}_{n}||^{2}=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{p_{n}}(\frac{\chi_{ni}(\alpha)}{\hat{\sigma}_{ni}}{-}\frac{W_{ni}(\alpha)}{\sigma_{ni}})^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{p_{n}}\Big\{\frac{W_{ni}(\alpha)}{\sigma_{ni}}\big[\frac{\sigma_{ni}}{\sigma_{ni}{+}O_{p}(a_{n})}-1\big]+\frac{\chi_{ni}(\alpha)-W_{ni}(\alpha)}{\sigma_{ni}{+}O_{p}(a_{n})}\Big\}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{2O_{p}(a^{2}_{n})\sum_{i=1}^{p_{n}}W_{ni}(\alpha)^{2}}{\sigma^{2}_{min}(\sigma_{min}{+}O_{p}(a_{n}))^{2}}+\frac{2||\boldsymbol{\chi}_{n}(\alpha)-\mathbf{W}_{n}(\alpha)||^{2}}{(\sigma_{min}{+}O_{p}(a_{n}))^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2O_{p}(a^{2}_{n})\sum_{i=1}^{p_{n}}W_{ni}(\alpha)^{2}}{\sigma^{2}_{min}(\sigma_{min}{+}O_{p}(a_{n}))^{2}}+\frac{O_{p}(b_{n})}{(\sigma_{min}{+}O_{p}(a_{n}))^{2}}.

(62)

Since
\sum_{i=1}^{p_{n}}W_{ni}(\alpha)^{2}\leq p_{n}\max\limits_{i=1,2,\dots,p_{n}}W_{ni}(\alpha)^{2}
, by Lemma
D.13
,
\sum_{i=1}^{p_{n}}W_{ni}(\alpha)^{2}=o_{p}(p_{n}^{1+\epsilon})
and
a_{n}^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{p_{n}}W_{ni}(\alpha)^{2}=o_{p}(\frac{p_{n}^{3+\epsilon}}{n})
for any small
\epsilon>0
. Since
\xi<\frac{1}{6}
, we have
n^{-1/4}p_{n}=o(1)
. Furthermore, withour loss of generality, assume
\Theta_{n}^{2}=p_{n}
, then
b_{n}=p_{n}\log(n)(\frac{p_{n}}{n})^{2\xi}\geq p_{n}\log(n)(\frac{p_{n}}{n})^{1/3}\geq\log(n)\frac{p_{n}^{4}}{n}
for large enough
n
, so the second term on the right hand side of Equation (
62
) dominates and
||\boldsymbol{\hat{P}}_{n}-\boldsymbol{P}_{n}||^{2}=O_{p}(b_{n})
.

On the space of cadlag functions
D[0,1]
, define
\chi_{ni}(r)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{t=1}^{{\lfloor nr\rfloor}}X^{i}_{nt}
,
\boldsymbol{\chi}_{n}(r)=(\chi_{n1}(r),\dots,\chi_{np_{n}}(r))^{\top}
and

Q^{(W)}_{n}(\alpha)=\frac{(1{-}\alpha)N_{Q}}{D_{Q}}=\frac{(1{-}\alpha)\Big\{\boldsymbol{{P}}_{n}^{\top}\big[\mathbf{W}_{n}(1){-}\mathbf{W}_{n}(\alpha)\big]\Big\}^{2}}{\int_{\alpha}^{1}\big\{\boldsymbol{{P}}_{n}^{\top}[\mathbf{W}_{n}(r){-}\mathbf{W}_{n}(\alpha)-\frac{r{-}\alpha}{1{-}\alpha}(\mathbf{W}_{n}(1){-}\mathbf{W}_{n}(\alpha))]\big\}^{2}dr}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}U_{1}.

(63)

If we can show
\frac{1}{n}\big\{\boldsymbol{\hat{P}}_{n}^{\top}\mathbf{S}_{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}+1,n}\big\}^{2}=N_{Q}+o_{p}(N_{Q})
and

\int_{\alpha}^{1}\big\{\boldsymbol{{\hat{P}}}_{n}^{\top}[\boldsymbol{\chi}_{n}(r){-}\boldsymbol{\chi}_{n}(\alpha){-}\frac{r{-}\alpha}{1{-}\alpha}(\boldsymbol{\chi}_{n}(1){-}\boldsymbol{\chi}_{n}(\alpha))]\big\}^{2}dr=D_{Q}+o_{p}(D_{Q}),

then we have
Q^{(D)}_{n}(\alpha)-Q^{(W)}_{n}(\alpha)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}0
and the theorem is proved. We divide the proof into two parts:

(a)
\frac{1}{n}\Big\{\boldsymbol{\hat{P}}_{n}^{\top}\mathbf{S}_{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}+1,n}\Big\}^{2}=N_{Q}+o_{p}(N_{Q})
.

(b)
\int_{\alpha}^{1}\big\{\boldsymbol{{\hat{P}}}_{n}^{\top}[\boldsymbol{\chi}_{n}(r){-}\boldsymbol{\chi}_{n}(\alpha){-}\frac{r{-}\alpha}{1{-}\alpha}(\boldsymbol{\chi}_{n}(1){-}\boldsymbol{\chi}_{n}(\alpha))]\big\}^{2}dr=D_{Q}+o_{p}(D_{Q})
.

Proof of part (a)
: Note that

\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\boldsymbol{\hat{P}}_{n}^{\top}\mathbf{S}_{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}{+}1,n}=\boldsymbol{{P}}_{n}^{\top}\big[\mathbf{W}_{n}(1){-}\mathbf{W}_{n}(\alpha)\big]+N_{Q1}+N_{Q2}+N_{Q3},

(64)

where
N_{Q1}=\big[\boldsymbol{\hat{P}}_{n}-\boldsymbol{{P}}_{n}\big]^{\top}\big[\mathbf{W}_{n}(1){-}\mathbf{W}_{n}(\alpha)\big]
,
N_{Q2}=\boldsymbol{{P}}_{n}^{\top}\big\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\mathbf{S}_{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}{+}1,n}-\big[\mathbf{W}_{n}(1){-}\mathbf{W}_{n}(\alpha)\big]\big\}
and
N_{Q3}=\big[\boldsymbol{\hat{P}}_{n}-\boldsymbol{{P}}_{n}\big]^{\top}\big\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\mathbf{S}_{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}{+}1,n}-\big[\mathbf{W}_{n}(1){-}\mathbf{W}_{n}(\alpha)\big]\big\}
. By the uniform boundedness of
\gamma_{ni}
,
\sigma_{ni}
and Lemma
D.13
, we have
||\mathbf{W}_{n}(1){-}\mathbf{W}_{n}(\alpha)||^{2}=o_{p}(p_{n}^{1+\epsilon})
,
||\boldsymbol{P}_{n}||^{2}=o_{p}(p_{n}^{1+\epsilon})
for any small
\epsilon>0
. By Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,
N_{Q1}\leq||\boldsymbol{\hat{P}}_{n}-\boldsymbol{{P}}_{n}||||\mathbf{W}_{n}(1){-}\mathbf{W}_{n}(\alpha)||=o_{p}(p_{n}^{1/2+\epsilon}\sqrt{b_{n}})
,
N_{Q2}\leq||\boldsymbol{{P}}_{n}||||\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\mathbf{S}_{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}{+}1,n}-\big[\mathbf{W}_{n}(1){-}\mathbf{W}_{n}(\alpha)\big]||=o_{p}(p_{n}^{1/2+\epsilon}\sqrt{b_{n}})
for any small
\epsilon>0
and
N_{Q3}\leq||\boldsymbol{\hat{P}}_{n}-\boldsymbol{{P}}_{n}||||\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\mathbf{S}_{{\lfloor n\alpha\rfloor}{+}1,n}-\big[\mathbf{W}_{n}(1){-}\mathbf{W}_{n}(\alpha)\big]||=O_{p}(b_{n})
. Since
p_{n}^{\epsilon_{1}}b_{n}\to 0
for some small
\epsilon_{1}>0
, all of
N_{Q1},N_{Q2}
and
N_{Q3}
are of the order
o_{p}(p_{n}^{1/2})
.

Next we prove
\big\{\boldsymbol{{P}}_{n}^{\top}\big[\mathbf{W}_{n}(1){-}\mathbf{W}_{n}(\alpha)\big]\big\}^{-1}=O_{p}(p_{n}^{-1/2})
. Denote
\mathbf{D_{g}}=Diag\{\frac{1}{\sigma_{n1}},\dots,\frac{1}{\sigma_{np_{n}}}\}
, note that
\boldsymbol{{P}}_{n}^{\top}\big[\mathbf{W}_{n}(1){-}\mathbf{W}_{n}(\alpha)\big]=\mathbf{B}_{p_{n}}(\alpha)^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{1/2}_{n}\mathbf{D_{g}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{1/2}_{n}\big[\mathbf{B}_{p_{n}}(1)-\mathbf{B}_{p_{n}}(\alpha)\big]
and let
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{n}=\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{1/2}_{n}\mathbf{D_{g}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{1/2}_{n}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{1/2}_{n}\mathbf{D_{g}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}^{1/2}_{n}
with eigenvalues
\lambda_{1}\leq\lambda_{2}\leq\cdots\leq\lambda_{p_{n}}
. We have
\mathbf{B}_{p_{n}}(\alpha)^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{n}\mathbf{B}_{p_{n}}(\alpha)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}{\alpha}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{p_{n}}\lambda_{i}\chi_{i}
where
\chi_{i}
are i.i.d chi-square distributed with one degree of freedom. Let
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{ni}
be the
i
th row of the symmetric matrix
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{n}^{1/2}
, since
\sum\limits_{i=1}^{p_{n}}\lambda_{i}=trace(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{n})=\sum\limits_{i,j=1}^{p_{n}}\{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{ni}^{\top}\mathbf{D_{g}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{nj}\}^{2}\geq\sum\limits_{i=1}^{p_{n}}\{\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{ni}^{\top}\mathbf{D_{g}}\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{ni}\}^{2}\geq\frac{\gamma^{2}_{min}}{\sigma^{2}_{max}}p_{n}
, by Lemma
D.19
, for
K>0
,

\displaystyle P(p_{n}\big\{\mathbf{B}_{p_{n}}(\alpha)^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{n}\mathbf{B}_{p_{n}}(\alpha)\big\}^{-1}\geq K)=

\displaystyle P(\mathbf{B}_{p_{n}}(\alpha)^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{n}\mathbf{B}_{p_{n}}(\alpha)\leq\frac{1}{K}p_{n})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle P(\sum\limits_{i=1}^{p_{n}}\lambda_{i}\chi_{i}\leq\frac{\sigma^{2}_{max}}{\alpha\gamma^{2}_{min}}\frac{1}{K}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{p_{n}}\lambda_{i})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{e}{{\alpha}}\frac{\sigma^{2}_{max}}{\gamma^{2}_{min}}\frac{1}{K}\Big)^{1/2}.

So
\big\{\mathbf{B}_{p_{n}}(\alpha)^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{n}\mathbf{B}_{p_{n}}(\alpha)\big\}^{-1}=O_{p}(p_{n}^{-1})
. Let
\Phi(\cdot)
be the cdf of standard normal distribution and note that conditioning on
\boldsymbol{{P}}_{n}
, we have
\boldsymbol{{P}}_{n}^{\top}\big[\mathbf{W}_{n}(1){-}\mathbf{W}_{n}(\alpha)\big]\sim N(0,(1{-}\alpha)\mathbf{B}_{p_{n}}(\alpha)^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{n}\mathbf{B}_{p_{n}}(\alpha))
, so

\displaystyle P(\big|p_{n}^{1/2}\big\{\boldsymbol{{P}}_{n}^{\top}\big[\mathbf{W}_{n}(1){-}\mathbf{W}_{n}(\alpha)\big]\big\}^{-1}\big|\geq K)=

\displaystyle E\Big\{2\Phi\big(\frac{1}{\sqrt{1{-}\alpha}}\sqrt{p_{n}\big\{\mathbf{B}_{p_{n}}(\alpha)^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{n}\mathbf{B}_{p_{n}}(\alpha)\big\}^{-1}}\frac{1}{K}\big){-}1\Big\}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\Phi\big(\frac{1}{\sqrt{1{-}\alpha}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{K}}\big){-}1{+}P(p_{n}\big\{\mathbf{B}_{p_{n}}(\alpha)^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}_{n}\mathbf{B}_{p_{n}}(\alpha)\big\}^{-1}\geq K)

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle 0\mbox{ as }K\to\infty,

(65)

which implies
\big\{\boldsymbol{{P}}_{n}^{\top}\big[\mathbf{W}_{n}(1){-}\mathbf{W}_{n}(\alpha)\big]\big\}^{-1}=O_{p}(p_{n}^{-1/2})
.

Proof of part (b)
: Denote
\boldsymbol{\tilde{\chi}}_{n}(r)=\boldsymbol{\chi}_{n}(r){-}\boldsymbol{\chi}_{n}(\alpha){-}\frac{r{-}\alpha}{1{-}\alpha}(\boldsymbol{\chi}_{n}(1){-}\boldsymbol{\chi}_{n}(\alpha))
,
\mathbf{\tilde{W}}_{n}(r)=(\tilde{W}_{n1}(r),\dots,\tilde{W}_{np_{n}}(r))^{\top}=\mathbf{W}_{n}(r){-}\mathbf{W}_{n}(\alpha)-\frac{r{-}\alpha}{1{-}\alpha}(\mathbf{W}_{n}(1){-}\mathbf{W}_{n}(\alpha))
,
\mathbf{\tilde{B}}_{n}(r)=\mathbf{B}_{n}(r){-}\mathbf{B}_{n}(\alpha)-\frac{r{-}\alpha}{1{-}\alpha}(\mathbf{B}_{n}(1){-}\mathbf{B}_{n}(\alpha))
,
D_{Q1}(r)=\boldsymbol{{P}}_{n}^{\top}\mathbf{\tilde{W}}_{n}(r)
,
D_{Q2}(r)=\big[\boldsymbol{\hat{P}}_{n}-\boldsymbol{{P}}_{n}\big]^{\top}\mathbf{\tilde{W}}_{n}(r)
,
D_{Q3}(r)=\boldsymbol{{P}}_{n}^{\top}\big[\boldsymbol{\tilde{\chi}}_{n}(r)-\mathbf{\tilde{W}}_{n}(r)\big]
and
